894.5
I have the honor to report that the Berlin DEUTSCHE
ALLGEMEINE ZEITUNG, January 9, 1945, published a tele-
graphic despatch dated Tokyo January 8, 1945, from its
Eastern Asia correspondent Wilhelm Schulze, on Japan's
financing of the war.
A translation of the despatch is
attached.
It reviews the new tax law, which is to be presented
to the Japanese Parliament in the second half of January.
645
The new tax law is expected to increase the yield from
taxes in 1945 by 21% compared to 1944. The most important
increases are on earned incomes from 15% to 18% and on
other types of personal incomes from 18% to 21%. The tax
on alcoholic beverages is to be raised, but is not
expected to increase revenue substantially, as all
alcoholic beverages are rationed. Entertainment taxes and
taxes on railway tickets are alsc to be inoreased.
Respectfully yours,
Munsthase
J.'Klahr Huddle
CS/D
Chargé d'Affaires a.i.

=== Page 3 === (confidence 0.98)
(Enclosure to despatch No. 10590, January 16, 1945,
American Legation, Bern.)
JAPAN'S FINANCING OF THE WAR.
by Wilhelm Schulze, Eastern Asia correspondent.
(Source: Berlin DEUTSCIE ALLGEEINE ZEITUNG, January 9, 1945)
Tokyo, January 8, 1945
The Japanese overnment has decided to submit a
supplementary tax law to Parliament, which will meet in
the second half of January. The aim of this supplementary
law is to raise the necessary funds for the conduct of the
war and to reduce the surplus purchasing power in order to
avoid dan erous tendencies in the Japanese monetary situa-
tion.
This is the ninth supplementary tax law since the
start of the war in China in 1937. Compared to 1944, the
ield rom taxes will increase by 21% in the financial
year 1945.
8
The most important provisions of the supplementary tax
law are the increases o tne normal income tax, the normal
corporation profits tax and a few indirect taxes. The tax
on earned incomes, which up to now has amounted to 15%,
after the deduction of certain exemptions and the amount
required for the support of members of the family, will be.
increased to 18. The tax on other kinds of personal
incomes will be increased from 18% to 21%. The tax imposed
on incomes from government loans will amount to 22% in
future, instead of 19% as at present. The tax rate imposed
6
on incomes resulting from dividends and real estate will be
increased from 20% to 23% and the nomal corporation profits
tax from 30% to 33%. The extraordinary corporation profits
tax, however, which ineludes war profits, will only be
increased from 20% to 22%.
These taxes which are to be collected, according to
the supplementary tax law, represent the main sources of
revenue of the Government, as the increase of the indirect
taxes, which is also planned by the Government, seems to be
only nominal.In this connection, the taxes imposed on all
alcoholic bevera es are once more to be increased, but an
increase in the yield remains theoretical, as all alcoholic
beverages are rationed in very small quantities.
A bottle of beer, for example, which up to now has cost
one yen, will cost two yen in future. A rown-up person,
nowever, receives only one bottle a month, and sometimes even
less as the breweries are working primarily for the army.
Thus the inerease of receipts from these taxes will be very
small. The price of a bottle of Japanese whisky of first
quality has been increased from 50 to 70 yen and whisky of
second quality from 36 to 50 yen, but for months already, no
whisky has officially been alloted. It seems that only the
tax put on rice wine will be profitable, because rice wine
has regularly been sold, although in small quantities. Up
to

=== Page 4 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
to the present, l.8 liters of this wine, first quality,
have cost 12. 30 yen
nd quality 8.30 yen. Now, these
two qualitieto
mplaned by  new quality which
in future will cost 15 yen. Sake d Japanese spirit of
third quality will cost 8 yen in future, namely an increase
in price of 70%.
The increase in the entertainment tax is more or less
nominal, as the number of theatres and cinemas, which are
still open, is very small compared to earlier times, and
the entrance cost has been considerably reduced, in order
to enable the poorer classes also to see the performances.
A tax of lo0% will be imposed on tickets which cost 1 yen,
150% on tickets up to 5 yen, and 200% on tickets which
cost over 5 yen.
The tax on railway-tickets will also be increased, but
not to such a large extent. lip to the present, e tax amount-
ing to ll.5o yen has been imposed on a railway-ticket from
Tokyo to Osaka.
The new tax will amount to 13 yen. This
measure will probably not greatly reduce the number of
Japanese passen ers, which is very large at present.

FINANCLAL DIRECTIVE POR HILITARY GOVERDMENT IN JAPAN
1. You will use supplementary military yen (Type "B") issued pursuant to
military proclamation (hereinafter referred to as supplemental yen) and regalar
yen currency in your possession. Regular yen currency will include curremsies
which are now legal tender in the area. Supplemental yen will be deelared legal
tender and will be interchangeable at par without distinction with regular yem
currencies. Records will be kept of the amount of such currencies used y your
forces.
2. Japanese military yen issued for circulation in territories oecupied
by the Japanese will not be legal tender within Japan and will not be acceptable
nor interchangeable with supplemental yen or regular yen currencies.
3. For military reasons, you will have the right at all times to use regular
or Hawaiian series U.S. dollars. You will, however, avoid the use of regular
or Hewaiian series U.S. dollars whenever possible and will resort to their use
only in emergencies.
4. If it is found necessary to use regular or Hawaiian series U.S. dollars, thy
following provisions will epply to such use:
a. A proclamation will be iasued requiring all persons to accept the
regular or Hawaiian series U.S. dollars at the established date. Transactions
8
at any other rate will be prohibited.
b. The issuance of regular or Hawaiian series U.S. dollars will cease
h
and supplemental yen and regular yen currencies will be issued in their place
as soon as possible.
c. Regular or Hawaiian series U.S. dollars will be withdram from cir-
culation as soon as such withdrawal can be satisfactorily accomplished.
d. Records will be kept of the emount of reguler and Hawaiian series
U.S. dollars used by your forces.
5. U.S. dollars (except as provided in paragraphs 3 and 4) will not be
permitted to circulete in the area. If you consider it advisable, you may require
all holders of U.S. dollars (unless it is found necessary to authorise their use

6. The provisional rate of exchange to be used exclusively for pay of
military and naval personnel and for military and naval accounting purposes will
be __ supplemental yen, and/or regular yen currency, to the .s. dollar.
Transactions at any other rate will be prohibited. A general rate of exohange
may be furnished to you later.
7. All foreign financial transactions of any kind, including all exports
and imports of currency and securities, will be prohibited, except as permitted
under regulations you may issue. Army Finance Officers and Navy Disbursing
Officers may, however, be authorized to accept U.S. dollars from personnel of
your forces, end from other authorized personnel, for conversion into supplemental
and/or regular yen currency at the established rate of exchange, after satisfying
themselves as to the source of such U.S. dollars. Except for military or au-
thorized personnel, no requests by holders of yen currency or deposits, for the
exchange of such currency or deposits for U.S. dollars or eny foreign currency
or foreign exchange, will be allowed except in accordance with official exchange
regulations promulgated by you after notification to and approval by the Joint
Chiefs of Staff.
8. The Financial Division of the Civil Affairs Section for Japen will in-
clude in its functions the control of all funds to be used by your forces within
the area other than U.S. dollars, the disposal of which will be under the control
of Army Finance Officers and Navy Diebursing Officers of your forces. It will
maintain such accounts end records as are necessary to indicate the supply, con-
trol and movement of all currencies including U.S. dollars if used, and other
funds, as well ae finencial data required for the determination of expenditures
erising out of operations and activities involving participation of your military

a. In so far as operations relate to the provision of yen currencies
for the pay end other cash requirements of military and naval components of your
forces, the Financial Division will supply supplemental yen or regalar yen our-
rency from currency on hand, and will record the debit against the military and
naval forces concerned.
b. In so far as operations relate to the provision of yen currensies
for civil administration, the Financial Division will supply supplementary yen
or regular yen currency from currency on hand and will record the debit against
the appropriate Japanese authority, edministration, or agency.
c. The records of the Binancial Division will indicate in all cases the
currency in which receipts were obtained or disbursements made by the Finanoial
Division.
9. When satisfied that eny bank or other financial institution is under
adequate control and supervision, you may use such bank or other financial in-
stitution for official business. You may designate under direct military control
branch
and eupervision the Bank of Japan, or any bank thereof, or any other suitable
bank, as agent for the Finencial Division of the Civil Affairs Section. It is
not anticipated that you will make credits available to the Bank of Japan or any
other bank. However, if in your opinion, such action becomes essential, you
should report the facts to the Joint Chiefs of Staff for further instructions.
10. You will put into effect the following financial controls:
a. Pending determination of future disposition, all gold, foreign cur-
rency, foreign securities, accounts in financial institutions, credits, valuable
papers and all similer assets held by or on behalf of the following will be im-
pounded or blocked end will be used or otherwise dealt with only as permitted
under licenses or other instructions which you mey issue:
(1) Jepanese national, state, provincial, and local governments,
and agencies end instrumentalities thereof.
(2) Other enemy governments, the egencies and instrumentalities
thereof, and their nationals.
(3) Owners and holders, including nentral and United Nations Goverm-
ments or national authorities, absent from the areas of Japan
under your control.
SECHET

SECRET
(4) All persons, political organisations and societies regarded
as hostile or dangerous to military security.
(5) Persons under detention or other types of custedy  the mill-
tary authorities.
b. No governmental or private bank or ageney will be authorised to issme
bank notes or currency except that you may so authorise the Bank of Japan whem it
is under adequate military control and mupervision.
c. No military pensions (except for physical disability limiting the
pensioner's ability to work) shall be paid.
1l. You should put into effect only such further financial measures as you
deem necessary for military reasons. Accordingly, you may at your discretion
take the following steps:
a. Place banks under such control as you deem necessary to insure that
adequate facilities for military needs will be provided and that instructions
and reguletions issued by military authorities will be fully complied with.
Banks should be closed only long enough to introduce satisfactory control,to
remove objectionable pereonnel, and to issue instructions for the determination
of accounts to be blocked under paragraph l0a above.
b. Close all stock exchanges end similar financial institutfens, for
such period as you deem necessary.
c. Issue a proclamation prohibiting or prescribing regulations regard-
ing transfer or other dealings in real estate and securities.
12. Basic accounting instructions, supplemental to the foregoing directive,
are contained in Annex "A" hereof.

MEMORANDUM FOR Mr. H. Freeman Matthews
OFFICE

BASIC ACCOUTITING INSTRUCTIONS
(Supplemental to Financial Directive)
1. Your Civil Affairs Section for Jepan will be so organised as to provide
for:
a. Centralized control of accounts and records of the Civil Affairs
Section.
b. Uniformity of accounting policies end procedures for the Civil Affairs
Section.
c. Appropriate supervision of major financial functions in such a
manner as to assure reasonable internal controls and safeguards, including the
separation of accounting controls from the function of handling cash.
2. The Civil Affairs Sction should, in so fer as practicale, incorporate
in its procedure standerds of personal accountability and personal responsibility
equivalent to those applicable to the militery and naval components of your forees.
3. You sh uld keep eppropriate centralized records of ell yen curreney made
aveilable to you by the Treasury Depertment or from other sources, for either
military or civil administration purposes. The emounts which you so receive
should be currently reported to the Wer and avy Department, as the case may be;
theo War or Novy Depertment will in turn report to the Treasury Department. All
yen currency required in the rea by your forces, ither for military or civil
administration purposes, will be obtained from the Financial Division of your
Civil Affairs Section for Jupan.
4. Yen currency advanccd by the Finencial Division of tho Civil Affairs
Section for Japan to army and navy disbursing officers will be evidenced by
receipts. In r spect to each advance for tho purpose of making such expenditures
in the area as are chargeable to War or Navy Department appropriations. the
Financial Division of the Civil Affeirs Section for Jepan will report the details
thereof by radio to the War or Navy Department, as the case may be which will
in turn report to the Treasury Department. In eddition, cach disbursing officer
receiving advances for such purposes will submit an independent report by radio
of the amount received by him to the War or Navy Department, as the case may be.

=== Page 22 === (confidence 0.99)
(Enclosure to despatch no. 108, January 30, 1945)
(from the American Embassy at Chungking, China )
RESTRICTED
(Condensed translation by American Coordinated
Translation Center, Chungking, of article from
October 28, 1944 issue of Toyo Keizai (Oriental
Economist), Tokyo. Paragranhs in quotation
marks are full translations while the rest of
the article is summarized.)
INFLATION
"Nowadays people are talking about inflation and it
is true that there is a marked inflation in the notes of
the Bank of Japan. At the end of September the note issue
reached the large sum of Yen 13,730,000,000. This is an
increase of Yen 1,090,000,000 over the weekly average of
Yen 12,640,000,000 during the 1ast 10 days of August.
The weekly average for the last 10 days of September
1943 was Yen 7,130,000,000; therefore, the present figure
is an increase of Yen 6,600,000,000 over the same period
of last year. In other words, there is an actual increase
of 92 percent."
"In addition to this monthly inflation there is a
tendency towards lowering the rate at which notes return
to the Bank of Japan which is making us take notice. That
is to say, the inflation notes issued at the end of the
month do not easily flow back to the Bank at the beginning
of the next month. Thus there is a tendency to let the
inflated notes remain in circulation. If this situation
continues to the end of the year, I (who not stated) am
afraid there will be an unusual inflation."
"The process of this inflation, it goes without saying,
begins with the swelling of public expenditure and the
dispersal of government capital. The overall dispersal of
government capital is inareasing month by month and year by
year. In the past the government capital was quickly
furnished by the purchase of bonds, But this has slowed
down recently. The bonds which are still unsubscribed and
which remain with the Bank of Japan are accumulating. Not
only that, but the money lent out by the Bank of Japen for
the purpose of forced subscription of bonds is also increas-
ing. At the end of September the Bank of Japan had on hand
Yen 9,530,000,000 of unsubscribed bonds. At the same time
the money lent out by the Bank of Japan reached the large
sum of Yen 5,610,000,000. This sum may well tell half of
the story of the inflation in the notes of the Bank of
Japan."
The reasons for inflation or the causes of inflation
are entirely natural. It is a part of wartime economic
life. The rate of production increase does not run parallel
with the rate of inflation. The price of goods is naturally
increasing. A sum of money allocated for a certain production
does

=== Page 23 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
does not result in im ediate production. Money given to
military supply production necessarily goes into the
hands of civilians who will spend the money for their own
needs or will return the money to the Bank in the form of
savings. With the high cost of living, more money is
needed in the market. Furthermore people are reserving
some of the cash on hand for emergency needs in case of
air raids.
The solutions to these problems are:
1. Be stricter and more careful in the use of
government carital;
2. Pay more attention to how the money is used
and watch where it is going and how it is
being spent;
3. Make sure of a just and eyuitable distribution
of income.

September 15, 1944
Dear Mr. Woods:
Reference is made to your letter of September ?.
1944, giving a short description of a plan to sell more
War Bonds.
The plan which your propose is, briefly, for this
Goveriment to assume the bonded debt enemy countries
owe to citizens of the United States and to pay the
accrued interest and principal of that debt out of the
German-owned assets in the United States which are
either plocked by the Treasury under Executive Order
8389 as  mended, or are vested by the Alien Property
Custodian.
There are two major difficulties with your plan.
First, Congress has not vet ecided upon the policy with
resrect to the post-war disposition of enely-owned assets
in the Uniter States; anr, second, the total assets owned
in Germany and claims asserted against Germany by United
Stotes exceeds considerably the German-owned
as=ets in the Uniter States.
This Departnent coul not embark upon a progrem such
as you sugrest until  ft-r Congression: 1 policy has been
cecided. Furthermore, in view of the disn rtiy in total
as-ets, to do rs you suggest woula be to giver prefer-
entiel treatment to certai rouns insofar as their
rights against Germany are concerned.
Sincerely yours,
Ted R. Gomble
National Director
War Finance Division

Secretary Morgenthau telephoned this morning to say
that under what was known as an "Informal Committee for
5
Germany", headed by Mr. Clayton as chairman, we got out
what everybody seemed to think was a pretty good directive.
He said he understood that there was now a group, not
9.
headed by Clayton, getting out a directive on Japan,
1
and that the Treasury Department, as well as the State
2
Department, was wholly on the outside. Mr. Morgenthau
54
said that the Treasury Department had a real interest in
this directive and he would appreciate it if I would make
9
some inquiries about the matter.
I told Seoretary Morgenthau that the directive was not
yet in final form and there would perhaps be modifications
before it was sent out anywhere. I inquired if there was
a financial angle in it, to which Mr. Morgenthau replied
that there was a financial angle in all of these questions
once we begin to occupy these countries, adding that the
Army has a strong finance section. Mr. Morgenthau said
that Treasury had been in on the direotive for Germany
and also on the one for Austria, and he wondered now why
they were being excluded regarding Japan. Secretary
Morgenthau said that they would like to get in on these
things
FLH
2545
liev
Ced.

=== Page 37 === (confidence 0.96)
-2-
things while they were still in the formative period.
To an inquiry of Mr. Morgenthau as to why Clayton was
not chairman of the group on Japan, I told him that
Mr. Clayton was not conversant with the Japanese situa-
tion, and that in order to solve all the problems re-
lating to this bituation, it was necessary to know all
about conditions in Japan, the psychology of the people
and all the rest of it.
I told Mr. Morgenthau that this matter would have
to be passed by our Staff Committee in the Department.
before we submitted it to anyone else. Mr. Morgenthau
said that he and his assistants would like to have a
look at it before it was too late. I said that nothing
had as yet crystallized and that the question would have
to be put to the War and Navy Departmants. I added that
we would let him have a look at the paper in due course.
1.0.
Joseph C. Grew

46 - 15th St.
Fond du Lac, Wisc.
July 26th, 45.
Hon. Alexander Wiley,
Senatorial Office Bldg.,
Washington, D. C.
Dear Senator Wiley:
On March 2lst you wrote me a letter with enclosures
from the State Dept. Under clip I am sending letter to
State Department and answering such questions as pertain
to my case.
I have been trying to get Mr. A. T. Brenner, the
present Trustee to write a letter, but no success, guess
he don't get anything but grief and therefore is not
interested. Althaigh he might have written you separately re
this matter. Is that possible?
I am enclosing a clipping from Colliers magazine.
I cannot see why we are not entitled to protection and
also why we cannot file a claim or lien against the frozen
funds. My belief is that we are merely getting or making
certain that these governments are forced to complete their
transactions. If you investigate you will also find that
our Government appointed Dawes and Young to O.K. these German
loans making it all the more reasonable to expect their help.
Please look over the material I sent the State Dept. I
am very hard-up as I cannot work the way I used to. I have
requested if possible to release the over-due interest money.
To go back to an early letter of yours in which you said
that only newspaper people could get names of German and
Japanese bond-holders - You can see what a pressure it would
be to get others to help us by writing letters to their
representatives. I donit see why a Senator could not get a
hundred or so names. Do you know G. A. De Witte, managing
editor of a Washington paper, could not he get us some
names. Mr. De Witte is a former Fond du Lac boy.
Thanking you for the interest you have shown.
Very truly,
(signed) Charles R. Woods.

(1) Two attached sheets show the information regarding these
bonds.
These bonds are all registered with the government on forms
issued for that purpose
(2) Value as listed according to trustee's report are mostly
the actual ∞st at time of purchase altho in some cases municipal
bonds of German cities were sold at a loss and the money reinvested
in German government
(3) Information given on sheets to further inform you
all these securities were purchased thru brokems on the open
market in the U.S.
(6) At all times these bonds were held in the U.S. and
were purchased purely as an investment for trust funds
(7) By far the greatest part of the bonds were purchased
after i929 by the trustee
(8) (9) Does not enter into our situation.
NOTE: I wish to state that bonds are not due as yet - but
when a bond does not pay interest it is considered in default.

in Geauga (?) Co., Ohio. His grandfather fought with the

STANDARD FORM NO. 64
TO
FN - Mr. McGuire
DATE: August 6, 1945
FROM
FE - Mr. Ballantine
SUBJECT:
Financial Directive for Japan
COMMENT
29 a. It is stated here that Bank of Chosen and
8
Bank of Taiwan notes are to continue to be a part of
0
the yen currency, while in 33 a (3) it is stated that
4
the offices of the Bank of Taiwan and Bank of Chosen
are to be closed. This appears to be inconsistent.
5
39 a (1). Would it not interfere seriously with
ordinary administrative functions (which we desire to
8
keep going) to "impound or block" all "property" of
the "national, prefectural and local governments", even
3
if property and assets are to be released under license
4
soon after impounding?
5
39 a (3). It may be undesirable, both for political
and economic reasons, to block or impound the properties
and assets of the Japanese Imperial Household. It will
be impossible to determine whether or not it will be
politically desirable to impound such property and assets
until further developments in the situation have taken
place. Economically, it would appear undesirable to halt
C
the activities of the Imperial Household Ministry, which
operates much as does a large business house in administer-
C
ing the Imperial properties, which consist principally
C
of forest land and shares in well-known concerns, such as
the Bank of Japan and Nippon Yusen Kaisha. To stop this
administration would throw thousands of Japanese out of
work and would apparently accomplish no useful purpose.
It is therefore suggested that the authority to impound
or block these properties be made contingent upon in-
structions from Washington.
39 a (5). There is no organization known as "National
Shinto" whose funds and properties could be impounded. How- 
ever, funds will probably be found belonging to the Bureau
of Shrines of the Ninistry of Home Affairs, the Bureau which
auperyises the National Shinto shrines at home and abroad.
5
RM/B
Such
8
Anal.
F.. Dickover
9
ROv.

=== Page 49 === (confidence 0.97)
- 2 -
Such funds should be impounded. Sectarian Shinto is
under the supervision of the Ministry of Education, and
as sectarian Shinto is classed as a religion, its funds
presumably cannot be impounded.
39 a (7). The objective of this article is not
understood.
40 a. This appears to be a very large order. Is
it believed that men will be available to carry it out?
Officers of FE will be glad to discuss these questions
with you any time on Tuesday morning.

=== Page 60 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure to despatch no. 148 dated December 29, 1945, from
the United States Political Adviser, Tokyo, Japan, on the
subject "Transmitting Copy of Law for the Establishment of
the Foreign Trade Fund".
Law for the Establishment
of the Foreign Trade Fund
No. XXVI
We hereby give Our sanction to the Law for the
Establishment of the Foreign Trade Fund approved by the
Imperial Diet and cause the same to be promulgated.
This twentyfirst day of the twelfth month of
twentieth year of Showa.
Signed:
Hirohito
Seal of Emperor
Countersigned: Baron Kijuro Shidehara,
Prime Minister
Viscount Keizo Shibusawa,
Minister of Finance
Sankuro Ogasawara
Minister of Commerce and
Industry
Law No. 53

=== Page 61 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
Article l. A foreign trade fund is hereby established for
the settlement of account due to the foreign trade by the Govern-
ment.
Article 2. This fund 1s fixed at ¥50,000,000 to be set
aside from the surplus due to the settlement of the fiscal year
of l944-45's accounts belonging to the special account for the
adjustment of the exchange and trade.
Article 3. In cases of this fund proving insufficient,
the Government, on the responsibility of the Exchange and Trade
Adjustment Special Account, may make a loan in order to tempor-
arily supplement the fund.
The loan specifies in the preceding paragraph, at the
latest, must be refunded in the following fiscal year.
The Government may appropriate the surplus of the Netional
Treasury, in stead of the loan specified in Paragraph 1, within
the fiscal year in view.
Article 4. This fund may be utilized for the acquisition
of various goods for the foreign trade, the foreign currency,
or different goods as may be specified by the Imperial Ordinance.
All businesses concerning the operation of this fund, as
specified by the Minister of Finance, shell be treated by the
Bank of Japan.
Article 5. The fund operation profit and the accompany-
ing miscellaneous revenue arising from the operation of this
fund, as well as the business expenses, the fund operation
commission, the accompanying various expenses and the fund
operation losses arising from the operation of this fund, are
placed under the exchange and trade adjustment special account.
In cases of necessity for the disbursement of the expen-
diture which, in accordance with the provision of the preceding
paragraph, is placed under the exchange and trade adjustment
special account in question, the Government may make a loan on
the responsibility of the same account.
Article 6. In cases of the property belonging to this
fund either being lost or reduced in value, the Government,
irrespective of the provision of Article 4 of the Exchange and
Trade Adjustment Lew providing for the establishment of the
special account for the exchange and trade adjustment and other
measures, must redeem such loss or decrease in value, either by
meens of the surplus arising from the settlement of account be-
longing to the exchange end trade ad justment special account,
or by means of this fund.
Supplementary Provision:
The present law shall come into force as from the day of
its promulgation.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
SUBJECT: Application of Emergency Financial Measures and Pro-
posed Tax Laws to Foreign Nationals
The United States Political Adviser has the honor to re
port that on March 2 this Mission forwarded to the Chief of
Staff of this Headquarters copy of a letter fron the Yokohama
Branch of this Office (a copy of which is enclosed), in which
Information was requested in order to be able to reply to in
quiries from American citizens in Japan who are not connected
with the occupation forces, whether the emergency financial
894.51/4-2546
Woleasedby
measures recently put into force by the Japanese Government
are applicable to American citizens.
There is enclosed a copy of a memorandum dated March 28,
Com,eaz,
1946. from the Chief of Staff replying to our inquiry. It is
stated in this reply that the emergency financial regulations
Was. Navy
are applicable to all "private foreign nationals (Allied as
well as non-Allied)", who were permanently domiciled in Japan
prior to September 1, 1945, and who are not "specifically
accredited by the Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers to
perform duties which he considers necessary". It is also
stated that Allied and neutral nationals who have been given
permission since September 1, l945, to enter Japan for purposes
of interest to the Allied nations "will be extended the same
privileges as military personnel and persons assimilated to
military personnel".
There is also enclomed a copy of our memorandum to the
Chief of Staff dated February 18, 1946, in which there are
presented questions raised informally with this Mission by
FILED
the Swiss Diplomatic Representative in Japan, and by Mr. Oscar
Morland, Chief Political Adviser to the Personal Representative
of the British Prime Minister in Japan. These matters had to
do primarily with the exemption of official and personal funds
of diplomatic officials of neutral gevernments and with the
question whether foreign firms or foreign nationals would be
exempt from the proposed new taxes and capital levy in Japan.
The Chief of Staff replied on March 4, 1946, in a memorandun,
copy of which is also enclosed, to the effect that the Japanese
Government has agreed to exempt "from deposit blocking" official
p
and personal funds of all duly accredited Allied and neutral
diplomatic and consular personnel. It was stated that in re-
S
y
gard to the question of exemptions of foreign firms or foreign
nationals from the proposed new taxes and capital levy. the
matter was still under consideration snd no decision has yet
DCR - OFD Unit
been
2

=== Page 72 === (confidence 0.98)
2
been reached. In this connection, it may be noted that this
Mission expressed previously to Scap the informal opinion that
the treatment of foreign firms and foreign nationals under the
proposed new tax laws was a matter which should appropriately
be referred to Washington for decision at a Governmental level.
especially in view of the probable international complicatioa.
Buch reference has been made by priority telegram of March 23
to the Chief of Staff for the Joint Chiefs of Staff.

citigens who are resident in Japan and who are not connected
with the Occupation Forces, asking if the new currency res-
trictions and freesing of bank deposits are applicable to
them. They point out that it would be impossible for them to

=== Page 74 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 2 to despatch No. 393 dated April 25. 1946, from
the United States Political Aariser for Jepan, Tokyo, on the
subject "Application of Emergency Financial Measures and Pro-
posed Tax Laws to Foreign Nationals".
COPY
GENERAL HEADQUARTERS
SUPREME COMMANDER FOR THE ALLIED POWERS
APO 500
28 March 1946
MEMORANDUM FOR: The United States Political Adviser
SUBJECT
"Freezing Regulations" as applied to American
Citizens.
In reply to your letter of 2 March 1946 concerning the
Japanese Emergency Financial Measures Ordinance and the exemp-
tions from it which may be granted American citisens in Japan
who are not connected with the occupation forces, the regu-
lations of the Japanese Government apply as follows:
a. Private Foreign nationals (Allied as well as non-
Allied) with permanent domicile in Japan established prior to
1 September l945 and who are not specifically accredited by
the Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers to perform duties
which he considers necessary, shall be subject to the provisions
of the Japanese Emergency Financial Measures Ordinance. Allied
and non-Allied nationals in the category of this paragraph
who are without current income from salaries and wages shall
be permitted to pay for any special food rations which the
Japanese Government may extend to them voluntarily or under
ScAP directive by withdrawal of cash from their bank accounts
or by use of restricted check.
b. Allied and Neutral nationals who have obtained permits
from General Headquarters, Supreme Commander for the Allied
Powers to enter Japan since 1 September 1945 for purposes of
interest to the Allied Nations will be extended the same privi-
leges as military personnel and persons assimilated to mili-
tary personnel.
(S.J.Chamberlin)
S. J. CHAMBERLIN
Maj. Gen., GSC
Acting Chief of Staff

=== Page 75 === (confidence 0.99)
Eaclosure No. 3 to despatch No. 393 dated April 25, 1946, from
the United States Political Adviser for Japan, Tokyo, on the
subject "Application of Mmergency Financial Measures and Pro-
posed Tax Laws to Foreign Nationals".
COPY
OFFICE OF THE UNITED STATES POLITICAL ADVISER
Tokyo, Japan, February 18, 1946.
MEMORANDUM FOR: Chief of Staff.
Mr. Pierre Micheli, Swiss Diplomatic Representative in Japan,
called this morning and asked the following questions in regard
to Japanese recent regulations concerning issuance of new Japanese
currency and proposed new taxes and a capital levy:
(1) How are the personal and official funds of neutral gov-
ornments and their officials to be converted and do the restric-
tive regulations apply to these funds?
(2) In view of the inability of foreign nationals to exist
in Japan on the small allowance of 300 Yen a month, are the re-
strictive regulations to apply to foreign nationals and foreign
firms?
(3) Are any exemptions to be made for foreign firms or
foreign nationals in the proposed new taxes and capital levy in
Japan?
With regard to the third question, Mr. Micheli pointed out
that Swiss firms in Japan during the war had been unable to con-
duct their business in a mormal manner and had been operating at
a loss. They were therefore in no position to meet the probable
high taxes without complete liquidation of their businesses. He
mentioned in particular the firms of Nichisui, Leibermann, Walchli,
Nestle S.A., all of Kobe; Siber Hegner, Kobe and Yokohama; and
Schulthess and Company (formerly Charles Rudolph), Yokohama.
We informed Mr. Micheli that, in accordance with his specific
request, we should be glad to lay these questions before SCAP.
but it was suggested that he should himself approach SCap through
the regular liaison chennels of G-2.
Mr. Oscar Morland. of the British Liaison Mission, has infor-
mally raised similar questions regarding British nationals resid-
ing in Japan.
Max W. Bishop
Foreign Service Officer in Charge
851.

=== Page 76 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 4 to despatch No. 393 dated April 25, 1946, from
the Umited States Political Adviser for Japan, Tokyo, om the
subject "Application of Emergency Financial Measures and Pro-
posed Tax Laws to Foreign Nationals".
COPY
4 March 1946
SUBJECT:
Exemption of Certain Categories of Funds from
Blocking.
MEMORANDUM FOR UNITED STATES POLITICAL ADVISOR.
1. With reference to your memorandum of 18 February 1946,
requesting information on exemptions from the deposit blocking
program, Imperial Japanese Government has agreed to the follow-
ing exemptions from deposit blocking:
a. Official funds of all Allied and neutral goverp
monts are exempt from blocking, and are permitted to be com
verted into or withdrawn in new currency.
b. Personal funds of duly accredited dtplomatic and
consular personnel, of Allied and neutral nations are also
exempted from blocking and may be withdrawn in new currency.
This exemption is strictly limited to foreign nationals on of-
ficial missions for their own governments and duly accredited
to ScaP: it does not extend to nationals of Japan or of a third
country working temporarily for a duly accredited foreign re-
presentative or mission.
c. Private foreign nationals with pormanent domicile
in Japan will be treated on the same basis as Japanese nation-
als, except that in those cases where a special food ration is
given to foreign nationals, either by the Imperial Japanese
Government on its own initiative or in compliance with SCAP
directive, permission will be given to withdraw funds necessary
to purchase the special ration in cash or by restricted check
as the case may require.
2. With reference to your question on exemptions for foreign
firms or foreign nationals from the proposed new taxes and capital
levy, this matter is still under consideretion and no decision
has yet been made.
(S.J.Chamberlin)
S. J. CHAMBERLIN
Major General, GSC
Acting Chief of Staff

A definitive statement regarding the future position

securities is primarily a private transaction into whioh
the purchaser voluntarily enters after calculation of
the risks involved. In the case of securities issued by
private entities, it is expected that holders will

My dear Mrs. Green:
Reference is made to your letter of August 13, 1946,
relative to certain 6-1/2% Imperial Japanese Government
bonds due in 1954, which you hold.
Apparently your inquiry was motivated by a Press
Release by the Department dated August 1, 1946. This
Release was intended to refer to equity interests in
Japanese companies rather than to bonds issued by the
Japanese Government.
A definitive statement regarding the future position
of Japanese Government dollar bonds is not now possible,
but the following may be helpful to your underatanding
of the Department's policy with reference to such obli-
gations.
The Department gonerally considers that investment
in foreign bonds is primarily a private transaction into
whioh the purchaser enters after calculation of the
risks involved. In case of default on dollar bonds
issued or guaranteed by foreign Governments, it is not
the practice of the Department to negotiate direotly for
American holders, but it does endeavor to render such
assistance as it appropriately can to them or their
representatives. In this connection your attontion may
be called to the Foreign Bondholders Protective Counoil,
Incorporated, 90 Broad Street, New York, New Tork, which
was established several years ago as a central repre-
sontative body. Although the Council was organized with
the encouragement of the Department, it acts on its own
responsibility. It is understood that the Council is
now interesting itself in such bonds as those held by
you, and you may wish to write to it in connection with
your holdings.
You
Mrs. Helen R. P. Green,
Rock Hall,
Maryland.

=== Page 83 === (confidence 0.97)
-2-

to state at the present moment what provisions, if any,
affecting such obligations may be included in the peass
treaty or what arrangement for prior payment.of claims
and demands arising from the war may be made. As you
probably kmow, there will be many such claims and demands
against Japan and against persons and municipalities
of that country, of which such bonds as those mentioned
by you will probably constitute only a small part.
Nevertheless, the Department will make a record,
for such consideration as may be appropriate, of any
information you may submit regarding your bonds. In
addition to the information contained in your letter of

and date of acquisition, the purchase price and the manner

In reply refer to

My dear Mrs. Ryugo:
Reference is made to your letter of August 24,
1946, relative to certain Imperial Japanese Govornment
and Tokyo Electric Light Company, Limited, dollar bonds
88
which you hold.
6
Apparently your inquiry was motivated by a Press
Release by the Department dated August 1, 1946. This
5
Release was intended to refer to equity participation
in Japanese business enterprises rather than to bonds
or other obligations of such companies or of the
8
Japanese Government.
1
2446
A definitive statonent regarding the future position
of Japenese dollar bonds is not now possible, but the
following may be helpful to your understanding of the
Departnent's policy with reference to such obligations.
The Department considers that investment in foreign
securities is primarily a private transaction into which
the purchaser voluntarily enters after calculation of
the risks involved. In the case of securities Issued by
private entities, it is expected that holders will
attenpt to secure coapliance with the contractual terms
through the usual channels when resort to such measures
becomes possible. This would apply to your Tokyo
lectric Light Company, Limited, bonds.
C
In the case of dollar bonds guaranteed by or isauod
by a governnental entity, a classification which would
72
include all of the bonds you hold, while the Department
does not negotiate directly on behalf of Anerican holdors,
it does endeavor to render such assistance as may be
appropriate to such holders or their ropresentatives. In
this
Mrs. Mutsu Ryugo,
Jait
5049 Kensington Avenue,
2
St. Louis 8, Missouri.
F
8

=== Page 96 === (confidence 0.98)
-8-
this conneetion, attention may be called to the Foreign
Bondholders Protective Council, Incorporated, 90 Broad
Street, New York, New York, established several years ago
to serve as a central representative body. The Council
was created with the encouragement of the Department, but
operates on its own responsibility. It is understood
that the Council is interesting itself in such securities
as the bonds held by you, and you may wish to write to
it in this connection.
I think you will appreciate that the Department is
unable to state at the present moment what provisions,
if any, affecting such obligations may be included in
the peace settlement, or what provision for prior pay-
ment of claims arising from the war may be agreed upon.
As you are no doubt aware, there will be many claims and
demands of different types against Japan and against
persons and municipalities in that country, of which
such bonds as those owned by you will probably constituto
only a small part.
However, the Department will be glad to make a
record, for such consideration as may be appropriate, of
any information you may send to it regarding your
holdings. This should include, in addition to the data
contained in your letter, the purchase price, the
manner and date of your acquisition of these securities,
the date of the last interest payment, and the manner
(whether by birth or naturalization) and date of your
acquisition of American citizenship. In case you acquired
citizenship by naturalization, the number of your
naturalization certificate and the name and location of
the court in which you wero naturalized should also be
stated.
If it should later be found appropriate for this
Government to take any action with respect to suah
obligations as those you own, due announcement thereof
will be made in order that interested American citizens
may be properly informed.
Sincerely yours,

Dear Sirs:
Under date of August 2, 1946 a notice appeared in the New York Times
894.51/9-
which stated that "American holders of securities or obligations of Japanese
business enterprises were urged today by the State Department to file state-
ments immediately with ite Division of Foreign Service Administration giving
information which will serve to protect their enterprises in the program of
monopoly diesolution being undertaken by the Allies in Japan."
There is some doubt regarding the interpretation of the term "obli-
gations". Does this refer only to bonds and/or securities of Japanese business
enterprises or may it be interpreted in its broader senee to cover ordinary
1046
debts owed by Japanese business enterprises which are documented by a signed
note, draft or other suitable inetrument?
While we do not hold bonds and securities of Japanese business
enterprises, we do have obligations of the latter type and would like to know
1f it is in order to file statements with your Division of Foreign Service
Administration in cover of such monies due us from Japanese commercial borrowers.

My dear Mr. Nakahara:
Reference is made to your letter of December 20,
1946, regarding dollar bonds of the City of Yokohama
and the Tokyo Electric Light Compary, Limited.
A definitive reply to your inquiry is not now
possible, but the following may be helpful to your
understanding of the Department's policy with regard
to such obligations.
The Department considers that investment in foreign
securities is primarily a private transaotion into which
the purchaser enters after calculation of the risks in-
volved. In the case of bonds issued by private entities,
as are the Tokyo lectric Light Company bonds, it is
assumed that holders will be prepared to avail themselves.
if necessary, of the usual methods to secure enforoement
of the contractual provisions, when resort to such
measures is possible.
In the case of obligations issued by, or guaranteed
by, governmental entities, --a category which would include
both the Tokyo Electric Light Company and the City of
Yokohama bonus, --it is not the practice of the Department
to negotiate directly for American holders, but it does
C
endeavor to renuer such assistance as it appropriately
IS
can to such holders or their representatives. In this
connection attention may be called to the Foreign Bond-
<
holders Protective Council, Incorporated, 90 Broad Stroet,
New York, New York, which was established several years
ago
Mr. Tsuyoshi Nakahara,
1031 King Street,
Seattle, Washington.
12-2046
R

=== Page 122 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2-
ago to serve as a central representative body. Although
the Council was organized with the encouragement of the
Department, it operates on its own responsibility. Several
months ago, at the request of the Counoil, the Department
transmitted to the United States Political Adviser to the
Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers, at Tokyo, a com-
plete statement of outstanding dollar bonds issued or
guaranteed by Japanese governmental entities. This state-
ment was forwarded for the information and use of the
Political Adviser and of the appropriate section of the
Staff of the Supreme Commander, and for such protection
of the American interest therein as might be appropriate
and feasible in the circumstances. The Department frequently
suggests to holders of such securities that they may wish to
communicate with the Council relative to their respeotive
holdings.
The Department is unable to state at the present time
what provisions, if any, affecting such obligations may be
included in the Peace Treaty with Japan, or what provi-
sion for clains arising out of the war may be made. As
you are no doubt aware, there will be many claims and
demands of different types against Japan, and against munio-
ipalities and persons in that country, of whioh such bonds
as those to which you refer will probably constitute only
a small part.
However, the Department is always glad to make a record
of any holdings of American citizens, for such considera-
tion as may be found to be appropriate, and suggests that
they send to it the pertinent details identifying and des-
cribing their bonds (name of obligor, dates ofissue and
maturity, interest rate, and currency in which payable),
the purchase price, the date and manner of purchase, and
the date and manner (whether by birth or naturalization)
of their acquisition of American citizenship.
If it should be found advisable that American holders
take further steps relative to such obligations, due announoe-
ment will be made in the public press.

My dear Mr. Masaeka:
Reference is made to your letter of Jammary 6, 1947, inquiring
as to whether asy procedures have been established for filing claims
to yen deposits in baaks in Japan which were established through
647
deposits in branches of such banks in this country. You inquire
further as to the possibility of filing a declaratiom of ownerahip
of such deposits at this time.
No proeedure has been establisbed by which claims for property
in Japan owned by persons in the United States ean be processed. You
are, of course aware, that no foreign exchange transactiom under whick
Japanese yen balances may be converted into U.S. dollars or other
foreign eurrency are possible at the present time. Japam has mo
foreign exchange assets available to effect such conversions, and even
after a commercial foreign exehange rate is established, an action not
foreseen in the near future, it is altogether likely that conversion
of yen balances not arising out of current foreign trade transactiems
will be subject to very comprehensive foreign exchange controls.
Inasmmch as the yen deposits to which you refer are property in
Japan owned or controlled by persons outside of Japan, such balances
are blocked by a directive of the Supreme Commander for the Allied
CS/V
Powers, to the Japanese Government, and any transaction or transfer
of such funds are prohibited at the prement time. You do not state
whether or not the owmers of these perticular accounts are citisens
of the United States. If so, their accounts would come under the
category of properties which the Japenese Geverament has been in-
structed to preserve and protect pending return to their owners. If
the

STANDARD FORM NO. 64
TO

DATE: January 7, 1947
FROM : OE - Mr. Whitman
suject: Army Department Pehabilitation Request for Japan,
1 Apr11 1948 - 30 June 1949.
Army and SCAP estimate that raw material and other Category B
requirements for a Japanese economic recovery program during the
15 month period beginning April 1, 1948, will amount in total to
452 million dollars. This is in addition to the GARIOA program for
disease and unrest imports amounting to about 497 million for this
period (FY 1949 GARIOA is 406 million). Of the 452 million for
rehabilitation, Army estimates that 272 million will be obtained from
bank credits based on the Japanese Export-Import Revolving Fund,
proceeds of exports, and barter transactions. It is planned that
Files
-untral
imports of raw materials to be manufactured into export goods, will
be financed primarily by the occupied Japan Export-Import Revolving
Fund, but that in addition, raw materials will be required to fill
the pipeline and to provide for goods retained in Japan for domestic
cnt Must be keneu
consumption.
The 180 million rehabilitation request is distinguished from the
GARIOA appropriation, primarily on the basis of types of materials
and services which may be purchased. E.g. about 350 million out of
the total of 452 million of expenditures on the rehabilitation pro-
ram will be for basic commodities, such as textile fibers, rubber,
woodpulp, etc., which, while they may in part be retained in Japan
for domestic consumpation, cannot be purchased with GARIOA funds.
Transportation costs, the purchase of certain end products from Army
surpluses in Japan, and the procurement of a limited amount of machinery,
etc., for Japanese industry, makes up the remainder of this program
in addition to basic raw materials.
According to the statenent which the Army proposes to make to the
Bureau of the Eudget and Congress, in support of the rehabilitation
request, the 180 million for this 15 month's period will enable Japan
to accumulate sufficient working capital in her export accounts to
abviate the need for future appropriations of this sort, and to stimu-
late exports to the extent required to enable Japan to achieve self-
support by the end of 1952. In addition, other appropriations osten-
sibly earmarked for relief expenditurcs, will be progressively reduced
by about 100 million per year during 1950, 1951 and 1952. The latter
C
assumption, however, is based not only upon an expansion of Japanese
exports, but upon an increase in short-term borrowings at the rate f
8
DO/R
roughly the amount by which relief expenditures are to be reduced.

My dear Mrs. Pulley:
Reference is made to your letter of January 12,
1947 relative to four 1000 Oriental Development
Company, Limited, bonds which you hold.
A definitive reply to your inquiry is not now
possible, but the following may be helpful to your
understandin; of the Department's position.
In general, the Department considers that invest-
ment in forein bonds is essentially a private trans-
action into which the purchaser voluntarily enters after
calculatin, the risk involved. In the case of obliga-
tions issued by private entities, it is assumed that
holders of such obligations will avail themselves of the
usual methods for enforcement of the contractual term
when resort to such measures becomes practicable.
In case of default on dollar bonds issued or
guaranteed by a government, while it is not the practice
of the Department to negotiate directly for American
holders, the epartment does enceavor to render such
assistance as it appropriately can to them or thoir
representatives. In this connection your attention may
be called to the  orein sondholders Protectiye Council,
Incorporated, 90 road treet, New ork, New Tork,
organized several years ago as a central representative
body. The Council was established with the encouragement
oi the uepartment, but acts on its own responsibility.
It is understood th t the touncil is now interesting
itself in tho situation of such bonds as those to which
you

CJM
Dear Ed:
Following the last talk I had with you and some
of your associates, as well as with the War Department, on
the subject of Japanese trade financing, I have been trying
89
to develop some thoughts for a more concrete program of
action. It is, of course, very difficult at this stage to
4.51/4-9
get down to anything like a specific proposal, mainly be-
cause so many of the essential elements in the situation
are as yet unclear. On the other hand, I believe that the
best way to clarify some of the open issues involved is to
start an exchange of ideas; with this in view I have dis-
cussed the matter with another banking institution - one of
the largest commercial banks in the country - which has ex-
pressed interest in joining us in the study and possible
47
handling of such financing.
In order to start the ball rolling I should, there-
fore, like to set out below a few lines of approach and sug-
gestions which I am, of course, making in a strictly informal
manner, in the hope that doing so may provoke further dis-
cussion and eventually lead to the initiation of more formal
negotiations.
I don't have to tell you that there are at present
four main reasons why it is difficult to contemplate a pri-
C
CS/A
vate credit arrangement to finance Japanese imports:
1.
We are as yet unclear as to identity, status
(legal and practical), and resources of the debtor
under such an arrangement.
2.
No one is going to be willing to lend dollars
unless he can see quite clearly where the dol-
lars are going to come from for the repayment
of the loan.
3.
The dollar famine throughout the world is in-
creasing rather tnan decreasing and countries
other than the United States will be more and
2年
more reluctant to pay for imports from Japan
in U.S. dollars.
4.
Congress appears to have been told that dollar
export proceeds will in the first instance be
used toward repayment of occupation costs,
7
FD 0
mainly food supplies.
5
f
2

=== Page 283 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
On the other hand, it seems to me that none of
these difficulties is really unsurmountable and what we
have got to do is to make a realistic appraisal of present
facts and figures and future prospects and then see just
exactly where private financing can come in.
I believe that the wisest thing to do would be
at first to try to set up something on a fairly small
scale so as to see how things work out in practice and
then try to enlarge the scope later on once some kind of
a record has been established. It would, furthermore, be
advisable to proceed from the start in such a manner as
would enlist the cooperation and interest of as many lead-
ing banks in various parts of the country as possible so as
to have a broad base on which to build for the future.
Here then, is what we have in mind at this stage
of our discussions:
A banking syndicate to be formed for the purpose
of arranging a credit in the amount of, say, $20,000,000
to $25,000,000 to SCAP directly, or to an agency of SCAP,
or to an agency of the Japanese Government under complete
SCAP control. Such a credit would be used exclusively for
the purpose of financing imports into Japan of cotton and
certain category B imports from the United States or from
any other country in the Western Hemisphere or from the
Philippine Islands. It would be utilized by availments
against shipping documents covering such imports into
Japan and could, of course, be drawn down in installments.
There would be a specific understanding that every avail-
ment would be repayable out of dollar proceeds of exports
of merchandise processed out of imports financed by the
syndicate, as this is thought to facilitate the granting
of a clear first lien on such export proceeds. In order
to make the operation attractive to the banking community,
each availment should be outstanding not more than 180 days,
possibly with the privilege of one renewal for 90 days
under unforeseen emergency circumstances.
As security for such a credit one might contem-
plate the following:
(a) A cash margin of 25% to be maintained
during the life of the credit, such
margin to be taken out of SCAp funds
or such other funds as the Var Depart-
ment may make available.

=== Page 284 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
(b) A first lien on the goods thus financed
while they are being processed in Japan,
under the trusteeship of SCAP.
of the lenders on the goods thus imported,
processed and exported.
(a)
A commitment from SCAP, given at the time
the credit is arranged, to establish a first
lien on, and a notified assignment of, pro-
ceeds of exports from Japan to the United
States or to countries other than the United
States but payable in U.S. dollars, sub-
ject to approval by the lenders in each
individual case of any country so involved.
(e)
Some assurance from the War Department that
in the event of unforeseen emergency condi-
tions (such as war or a prolonged production
stoppage in Japan or some other force majeure
occurrence) the War Department would take
appropriate steps to safeguard the ultimate
repayment of any availments outstanding.
I repeat that the above merely represents a sug-
gested basis for further discussion to which I should like to
get your reaction. I shall be in Washington for a few days
during the latter part of this week and shall be ready to
explore the matter further with you and your associates in
the State and War Departments. In the meantime, I am sending
copy of this letter directly to Colonel Gilchrist.
Sincerely,
Bore
Norbert A. Bogdan
Vioe President
Edwin M. Martin, Esq.
Chief, Division of Japanese & Korean Economic Affairs,
Department of State,
Washington, D. C.

OF STATE
April 9, 1947
Dear Jaek:
Friends and clients of ours, Messrs. Hart Ine. in
Boston, have just been in to see me with te following prepo-
sition. By way of introduction let me say that this is s
well estublished firm of wool merehants in Bostom, of Dutch
origin, with offices in Argentina, Uruguay, Australia, Holland
and recently I also believe in Africa. They are at the top
of the list of wool shippers from South Amerioa at the present
time and command excellent standing in trade circles.
Bhat they propose is to form a small group of three
to four wool houses for the purpose of shipping $3,000,000 to
$5,000,000 worth of raw wool from South Amri and Autralla
to Japan for combing and spinning into yarm. fhis wool would
be turned over to Japanese mills "in commissiem", 1.e. it
take the yarn out of Japan and sell it in werld markets (out-
side the United States). Por all expenses imeurred in Japan
(such as freight, labor, profit to the mills ete.) the group
would pay not in yen or in dollars but in raw wool. The not
result of the transaction, as far as Japan is concerned, would
be the sale of labor by Japan against payment in wool which in
turn means foreign exchange to her. The whole transaetion
would of course have to te carried out under the trusteeship
and supervision of SCAP as the wool people here would want
somebody to see th.t instructions are complied with, schedules
adhored to etc.
Messrs. Hart have been doing this kind of business
with Italy, Holland and Germany for some time and are, of
course, well experienced in this field. Italy has recently
stopped the issuance of permits for these processing opera-
tions ed this 1s probably why Messrs. Hart are now anxious to
develop the Japanese angle.
Such financing as may be necessary to swing a deal

FINANCIAL STATEMENT ON COMMODITY CREDIT
CORPORATION COTTON ADVANCES TO JAPAN
The Commodity Credit Corporation (CCc) had, as of March 31, 1947,
delivered about 452 million pounds of raw cotton valued at $122.03 million
1
f.a.s. US ports to the US Commercial Company (USCC) for shipment to Japan.
The accrued interest on these shipments by the end of March was $1.93 million,
bringing total CCC advances to 123.96 million. As shom in Table 1, CCC
accounts receivable from the sale of cotton textiles processed from advanced
ravw cotton amounted to 2.33 million, resulting in a net advance of $121.63
million. UScc advances for freight and insurance for cotton shipped have
been covered from the proceeds of cotton textile sales.
The above data are extracted from the financial statement of the
US Commercial Company dat ed March 31, 1947. However, since title to raw
cotton shipped to Japan does not pass to the Japanese agency until delivered
ex-vessel Japanese port, it is of significance to note financial data shown
in Table 2 on cotton reported as delivered to Japan. Data in Table 2 on
advances for raw cotton delivered to Japan have been extracted from other
UsCc material. Receipts from sales of cotton textiles and disposition of
net receipts have been selected from Table 1 (based on official financial
1. Iinder the terms of the State-Mar-Agriculture-USCC contract dated
February 7, 1946, covering export of raw cotton to Japan and ex-
port from Japan of textiles produced from CCC cotton.

statement showing disposition of receipts). Thus, met USCc advanoes
shown in Table 2 are the result of deduoting from landed cost of
cottom reported as delivered to Japan the expenses of the USCC for
cotton shipped although not necessarily received in Japan by March
31, 1947. Accordingly, on the basis of the value of raw cotton
delivered to Japan (about 420 million pounds by the end of Maroh),
CCC gross advances total $114.96 million and net advances $112.63
million, the CCC having been credited with $2.33 million from the net
proooeds of cotton textile sales.

FROM :
SUBJECT:
Japanese Securities and Exchange Law
The two basic objectives of any modification of the Japanese
law on corporate activities should be (1) the fullest practicable
disclosure of corporate activities and (2) more effective control
by stockholders over management. These two policies are corollaries
to the deconcentration and decartelization of Japanese industry.
The basic philosophy of any modification of the Japanese company
law should be oriented towards protecting not only stockholders
and creditors, but also towards preventing the resurgence of
zaibatsu control with all its attendant undesirable business
practices. The revision of corporate law to these ends has been
adopted by SWNCC as the policy of the United States Government.
In this respect the February 1947 Japanese Securities and
Exchange Law is designed solely to protect stockholders and
creditors. It is quite obvious that the Japanese public and the
world at large can be injured by corporate policies or actions
which benefit a company's stockholders and creditors. This is
particularly true of cartel arrangements.
It would seem to have been more advisable to have enacted
894.
one law in Japan similar to the British Company Law, 1929,
embracing both the objectives mentioned above. Japan, like
England and most of the other countries of the world, is not
hampered as is the United States by having numerous jurisdictions
each with its separate incorporating statute. The creation and
operation of business entities in Japan is controlled by the
Japanese Commercial Code (Japan is a Civil Law country), and
it is believed that there is no legal obstacle to enacting
one basic company law including the two objectives.
One or more experts, presumably.from SEC, should be sent
to Japan as soon as possible to supervise the drafting of a new
law or, if it should be decided not to enact a basic law, to
amend the present law and to prepare "ordinances" (regulations)
implementing the Securities and Exchange Law. (These ordinances
would presumably eminate from the Ministry of Finance.) This
law is relatively brief (92 articles) and is apparently not
intended to become operative until implemented by ordinances
inasmuch as 33 provisions contained in 26 of the articles
specifically

=== Page 332 === (confidence 1.00)
-2-
specifically require such implementation. 1/ The law itself
has apparently been drafted rather hastily, for, even discounting
distorted phraseology presumably resulting from translating
the original Japanese text (the official text) and the numerous
grammatical and typographical errors, there are omissions,
duplications, errors, ambiguities, and unwise provisions which
will have to be remedied.
Omissions
Article 6 contains examples of the type of omissions which
would appear to render the Japanese law rather ineffectual.
Section 9 of Article 6 provides that the statement to be submitted
to the Government as a prerequisite to the flotation of securi-
ties shall contain "matters to be stated on the prospectus."
Inconceivable as it may seem, nowhere in the law is there any
description of what is to be included in a prospectus, and in
addition it is implied in Sections 9 and 10 2 that the contents
of a prospectus are not to be subject of an ordinance. Obviously,
if the law is not to be amended or repealed, an ordinance will
have to be issued setting forth the information to be included
in a prospectus.
In
1 Attached to this memo is a summary of the articles requiring
implementation. This summary should make it clear that the scope
of the ordinances is sufficiently broad and complex to justify
assigning an expert to Japan.
2 1. Objective of company, trade name and matters pertaining to
capital or subscription.
2.
Business of company.
3.
Business result of company for the latest three business
years.
4.
Matters pertaining to property of company.
5.
Stock value of company at the end of the latest three years.
6. Plan on the use of funds raised as a result of the issue
of stocks or debentures concerned.
7. Types, descriptions, quantity and value of stocks or
debentures concerned.
8. Conditions for flotation or entrusting of flotation of
stocks or debentures concerned.
9.
Matters to be stated on the prospectus.
10.
Other matters as may be prescribed by an ordinance. (Emphasis
added.)

=== Page 333 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
In both the Securities Exchange Act of 1933 (Section 10)
and in the Company Law, 1929 (Sections 34-38 and the Fourth
Schedule) the information which must be included in a prospectus
is set forth. Similar requirements should have been included
in the Japanese law.
The other sections of Article 6 (i.e., of the first eight
sections) contain the type of information required on the
registration statement. When compared to the comparable pro-
visions of the Securities Act (Schedule A) or the Company Law
(in Britain the prospectus serves also as a registration state-
ment), the Japanese law seems hopelessly inadequate. These
eight sections are so brief that if effective disclosure is
to be enforced and if Japanese corporations are to be given
a fair definition of the scope of the information that they are
required to divulge, considerable study and care should be taken
in drafting the ordinance provided for in Section 10. This
ordinance assumes greater importance when it is realized that
in the eight sections, comprising as they do only 14 lines,
an attempt was made to establish requirements comparable to
those spelled out in 208 lines of the Securities Act.
Duplications
The following examples of duplication in the Japanese law
is given to illustrate the careless draftsmanship. Article 18
provides "if the amount of net assets of a securities dealer
happens to be smaller than the amount designated by the Govern-
ment in accordance with paragraph 1 of Article l6, the Govern-
ment shall immediately suspend his business." In Article 42
it is stated "if the amount of net assets of a member happens
to be smaller than the amount designated by the Government,
the Government shall immediately suspend 3 transactions on the
securities market and at the same time notify the securities
exchange of such fact." These two articles coulu very well have
been combined.
In Article 25 it is provided that securities dealers shall
"as may be prescribed by an ordinance, prepare a document
pertaining to the business and state necessary particulars there-
on." Not only is this provision objectionable for indefiniteness,
but
3 The word "his" has apparently been inadvertently omitted, for
as the provision now reaas all transactions on the exchange would
be stopped by the failure of one member to maintain his net assets
at the prescribed level.

=== Page 334 === (confidence 0.99)
-4-
but also it would seem to duplicate Article 27 which requires
dealers to prepare and file an annual "business report...as
may be prescribed by an ordinance."
Errors
Article 38 begins "should the license as prescribed by
paragraph 1 of Article 31 be obtained..." Article 31 does not
refer to licenses, and Article 32, to which the quoted section
apparently refers, contains only one paragraph.
Unwise Provisions
Inadequate investigatorial powers were delegated to the
Securities Transaction Commission. The carelessness of the
draftsmanship is exemplified in the phrasing of Section 3 of
Article 78, which contains the kernel of the powers of the
Commission; the other sections merely authorize the Commission
to "deliberate", "review", "make public", and "participate".
Section 3 empowers the Commission "to make investigations on
matters relating to enforcement of this law and, if deemed
necessary, to summon interested persons or to request 4/ the
submission of documents and books." (Emphasis added.) It is
difficult to perceive the logic in distinguishing between
witnesses and docunentary evidence in this provision. The section
authorizes the summoning of witnesses (presumably with the corollary
power to punish for contempt), but it only authorizes the Com-
mission to "re uest", but not to require, the submission of
documentary evidence. In answer to the possible contention that
"request" actually is intended to mean "require", it should be
pointed out that our courts, and presumably also the courts of
most foreign countries, usually give a narrow, and not a broad,
construction to delegations of quasi-judicial authority to an
administrative agency.
Under the Securities Exchange Act of 1934 the SEC is given
rather wide, but quite carefully delineated, investigatorial
powers. The SEC "may, in its discretion, make such investigations
as it deems necessary to determine whether any person has violated
or is about to violate any provision of this title or any rule
or regulation thereunder...any member of the Commission or any
officer
4 It is possible that "request" is an inaccurate translation of
the (official) Japanese text. In the Oriental Economist of May 24,
1947 (published in Japan), the word "demand" is used. The phraseo-
logy in the magazine is quite different from the text transmitted
to the Department.

=== Page 335 === (confidence 0.99)
-5-
officer designated by it is empowered to administer oaths and
affirmations, subpena witnesses, compel their attendance, take
evidence, and require the production of any books, papers,
correspondence, memoranda, or other records which the Commission
deems relevant or material to the inquiry...In the case of
contumacy by, or refusal to obey a subpena issued to, any person,
the Commission may invoke the aid of any court of the United
States...in requiring the attendance and testimony of witnesses
and the production of books, papers, correspondence, memoranda,
and other records. And such court may issue an order requiring
such person to appear before the commission or member or officer
designated by the Comaission, there to produce records, if so
ordered, or to give testimony touching the matter under investiga-
tion or in question; and any failureto obey such order of the court
may be punished by such court as a contempt thereof."
In view of the desirability of encouraging in every way
the disclosure of Japanese corporate activities and of the policy
of preventing excessive concentrations of economic power in Japan
it would appear illogical and contradictory to grant more
restrictive investigatorial powers to the Japanese Comaission
than are given to the SEC.
Attachment:
Summary.
5 The Japanese law fails to provide for judicial enforcement of the
Commission's rulings and orders. This of course will have to be
added, presumably by law, for it is very doubtful whether such a
provision could be included in an ordinance.

SUBJECT: Report Prepared by the Bank of Japan Entitled "Establish-
ment of Self-Supporting Economy and Credit".
GIVISION OF FRAANCIAL AFFAMRS
The Honorable
SEP 1 6 1947
The Secretary of State,
Washington.
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
Sir:
'oep
I have the honor to enclose one copy of a recent report entitled
"Establishment of Self-Supporting Economy and Credit", presented to
this Mission informally by ICHIMADA, Hisato, Governor of the Bank of
Japan. In view of the mumber of statements appearing in the Japanese
894.51/9-347
press concerning Japan's need for foreign loans, it is felt that the
section of the report dealing with international credits is of timely
interest.
An assumption is made in the report that in order to maintain the
average living standard authorized by the Far Eastern Commission (1930-
1934) an industrial level equivalent to that of 1936-1937 will be
necessary. Although the report fails adequately to explain the basis
894.
for such an assumption, it may be justifiable in view of such factors
as the following: the increased population of Japan; the loss of
revenues from "tourism", shipping, insurance, transportation, remittances
from relatives overseas, and other invisible exports; and the loss of
relatively cheap sources of supply.
Attention is invited to the industrial statistics quoted in the
report. Production and consumption figures for 1936-1937 appear inflated
in comparison with similar statistics given in the Japan Year Book, 1947
(published by the Foreign Affairs Association of Japan), and the Japan-
Manchukuo Year Book, 1940 (published by the Japan-Manchukuo Year Book
Company, Tokyo).
Respectfully yours,
Coittmin Benee
CS/JEC
William J. Sebald
Counselor of Mission

economy was cble to surmount with strength of its own,
end further mede it clear that foreign assistance
would be absolutely necessary for Japan to attain an
eccnonio recovery.

(4) Employment
In the last place, we wish to review the em-
ployment level under stabilized economy. In order
to realize the stabilization of economy in the real
sense, it is absolutely necessary to effect the full
employment. The production scale for the maintenan-
ce of the full employment would be set on the basis
of the 6o-million-ton coal production, on the as-
sumption that the productivity of labor would re-
cover to the level or the 1930-35 period. However,
what is meant in the "stabilized economy" mentioned
here is the minimum scale as explained above and
its economio activities are limited to around 70
per cent of those of the 1935-37 period. It is
impossible, therefore, to expect the realization
of the full employnent.
Estimating the tot al populat ion under the stabi-
lized economy at 79, 46c, v00, the number of population
employed is est imated at 52,17, 000 or 40 per cent
of the total population, on the assumption that the
aforesaid productive scale and the average labor
productive capacity in the 193e-35 period would be
attained. The population employed will include
