STANDARD PORM NO. 64
TO
: Mr. Havelick - OFD
DATE: September 17, 1947
Mr. Saltzman - SA
RNN
FROM : Mr. Whitman - OE
SUBJECT: Arrangements For Securing Bank Credits To Finance
Japanese Imports.
1. The FEC by policy decision of 23 July, 1947 (FEC 032/25), made
certain stocks of precious metals and other convertible Japanese-owmed
assets available for assisting in the financing of Japanese foreign trade
programs. Pursuant to this authorization, SCAP has established an Occu-
pied Japan
Export-Import Revolving Fund, the initial assets of which
will consist principally of these convertible assets of a value approx-
imating 137 million dollars, which will subsequently be supplemented by
the net proceeds of Japanese exports. The purpose of this revolving fund
is to serve as a credit base for bank loans to be used in financing self-
liquidating import-export programs in Japan, and for the direct purchase
of raw materials to be used in such programs. This memorandum is to bring
certain problems connected with the implementation of the FEC policy and
the SCAP program to the attention of officers of the Department concerned
with the policy aspects of the matter, and with representing the Depart-
ment on the Board of the Export-Import Bank.
6
2. War and State Department representatives have discussed several
different proposals of U.S. banks, in respect to the securing of loans
under the general provisions of the revolving fund plan referred to above.
It has been decided that the first program to be financed under this pro-
C
gram should be one for the financing of raw cotton imports from the U.S.
to supplement supplies now being secured from the Commodity Credit Cor
poration, which has supplied Japan with her raw cotton requirements during
the first two years of the occupation. The plan proposed for obtaining
bank fihancing of this raw cotton involves the participation by the Export-
Import Dank to the extent of approximately 25 percent of the total of the
60 million dollar credit which is considered as the maximum amount neces-
sary to have outstanding at any one time in order to maintain production
in the Japanese cotton textile industry. The plan for carrying out this
cotton financing arrangement, involves the establishment of the North
American Cotton Corporation (NACC) which would be an open-end corporation,
the capital of which (approximately $3,000,000) would be subscribed by
the cotton shippers, and which would be the acceptor of 9-month drafts
drawn by the shippers on the landing of the cotton in Japan. The con-
troller of the proposed revolving fund, on behalf of SCAP, would be res-
ponsible for putting NAcc in funds to pay the shippers' drafts at or
before maturity date. The revolving fund would be reimbursed from the
proceeds of the sale of cotton textile exports manufactured from cotton
secured under this arrangement. The gold and other assets in the fund
would be the ultimate security to the banks, and the Export-Import Bank
which would buy the drafts which had been accepted by NACC.
3. In

=== Page 423 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
3. In addition to the initial credit which it is hoped to arrange
with Export-Import Bank's participation for financing the raw cotton pro-
gram, it is hoped to secure a general line of credit for SCaP directly from
a consortium of private American and foreign banks. It is tentatively pro-
posed that this general line of credit be used in the first instance to pay
off outstanding obligations to the CCC for raw cotton previously and cur-
rently being supplied to Japan, possibly to liquidate drafts held by the
Export-Import and private banks under the cotton financing arrangement
outlined in 2 above, and to procure other raw materials to be used for self-
liquidating production programs in Japan.
4. In the course of discussions with the Export-Import Dank's repre-
sentatives, the cotton shippers, and representatives of private banks,
certain questions have arisen in connection with the basic documents on
which the program outlined above is based. These documents are the FEC
policy statement authorizing the use of the Japanese-owned convertible assets
for trade purposes (Attachment 1), and SCAP Circular No. 9 implementing this
authorization (Attachment 2). In respect to the FEC authorisation, the
principle question relates to the authority of SCAP to pledge the value of
the convertible assets as security for specific loans, or for a general
line of credit. This question arises because of the provision in the FEC
policy statement that the value of the assets should be preserved for repara-
tions, even though they may intially be used to acquire foreign exchange for
production programs. The Legal Advisor's Office of the State Department
has rendered an opinion that the pledge of these assets for credit purposes
is permissible under the terms of the FEC policy statement (Attachment 3).
It is considered that the terms under which the assets are to be utilized
as set forth in SCAP Circular No. 9, provides adequate safeguard against
the dissipation of the assets, and that unless such use is possible, the
FEC policy statement is impracticable of implementation. The Legal Advisor's
Office has also expressed the opinion that the assets in question may be
removed from Japan for safekeeping without liquidation, or may be pledged
in Japan with a committment by the SCAP that he will remove the assets from
Japan in case a political emergency renders such action necessary.
5. At the time the FEC policy statement was under consideration by the
FEC, the British representative on the Steering Committee of the Commission
expressed some doubt as to the possibility of using the assets in a "trading
account". He apparently felt that such use might be incompatible with the
"preservation of the value" thereof. However, in later discussions with
representatives of the State Department, British representatives have ex-
pressed the view that the revolving fund arrangement as set forth in SCAP
Circular No. 9, provides adequate safeguards against the dissipation of
the convertible assets.
6. A further question may be raised having to do with the priority
claim against these assets by trade creditors, such as the Export-Import
Bank or private banks on the one hand, and the U.S. Government for the
recovery of its occupation costs in Japan, and of other governments as
reparations claimants on the other hand. It is considered that the author-
ization by the FEC to utilize these assets for trade purposes clearly
iplies

=== Page 424 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
implies that any claims against the assets in connection with trade opere-
tions will have a prior claim on such assets. This will be explicitly set
forth in the U. S. position on occupation costs which is currently being
prepared for presentation to the FEC. Even without this additional support,
however, it is considered that the protection to trade creditors is ade-
quate under the terms to the FEC policy statement, and of SCAP Ciroular
Ño. 9.
7. Representatives of private banks interested in participating in
the proposed consortium, have expressed even more concern about the location
of the convertible assets themselves than in the legality of SCAP's authority
to pledge and liquidate the gold for trade purposes. The bankers feel that
if the gold, etc. remains in Japan, considerable danger exists that it will
not be available to meet any claims, not otherwise satisfied, that they may
have because;
a. Domestic upheaval or foreign invasion might result in loss of
the oollateral;
b. Legal access to the collateral would be difficult if it is not
located within the jurisdiction of a U.S. court;
c. Assets in Japan would be more accessible to reparations claim-
ants which might bring political pressure to bear on the U.S. to use
the assets for reparations, if they are still in Japan, and such
pressure might be exerted at a time when the U.S. would find it dif-
ficult to resist.
In view of these misgivings, the bankers have stipulated that loans will be
made against the assets in Japan, only at a rate of interest of 3 to 4
peroent, and on condition that;
a. Unequivocal legal opinions be secured regarding SCAP's auth-
ority to pledge the assets, and dispose of the collateral to meet
claims of the banks;
b. SCAP undertake to move the collateral from Japan, in event
of emergency such as imminence of war, or internal disturbance, or in
event such a drastic decline in world prices occurred that the neces-
sity of selling the collateral to meet outstanding claims became
apparent;
c. A debt ceiling of 100 percent of the value of the assets be
established.
These conditiops have,been commnicated informally to the State Depart-
subsequent credit extensions will depend to some extent on experience with
the cotton credit. If the assets, or sufficient of them to cover outstand-
ing utilized credits, were transported to the U.S., the banks have indicated
they would lend at 2 to 22 percent under the terms of SCAP Circular No. 9,
without as
comprehensive

=== Page 425 === (confidence 0.99)
-4-
Comprehensive a legal opinion as otherrise. This is probably due to the
legal protection they feel they would obtain from having the gold within
the jurisdiction of a U.S. court. The Department has the question of the
political implications of moving the gold under consideration at the
present time.
8. A further related problem is that of considering the adequacy and
clarity of SCAP Circular No. 9, both for pruposes of the above program,
and in relation to other programs such as the so-called "crank-up" program
for the recovery of Japanese foreign trade. It is considered that certain
changes (Attachment 4), in the Circular, are desirable to make for greater
flexibility in the use of the assets of the fund. These changes have been
discussed with certain representatives of the private banks who did not
consider they would reduce the willingness of the banks to extend credit
under the Circular. These changes have been communicated informally to the
War Department. While Var Department representatives have expressed agree-
ment with the intent of these revisions, they have thus far been averse to
making changes in SCAP Circular No. 9 at this time. State Department repre
sentatives, on the other hand, consider that any changes deemed desirable,
should be made prior to incorporation of the Circular into contracts for
loans to Japan.
9. The present status of the proposed cotton credit in the Export
Import Bank, appears to be that no formal action will be taken at the Board
of Directors level prior to receipt of a legal opinion from SCAP on his right
of disposition of the convertible assets. This opinion is expected shortly
from Tokyo.
10. It is recommended that:
a. The State Department representative on the Board of the Export
Import Bank endeavor to expedite the consideration of this matter by
the Bank, particularly by urging that the Bank formulate any requests
for further legal or factual information required from the Department
of State as soon as it is practicable to do so.
b. A decision be reached by the Department, in viewof the facts
cited in paragraph 7 above, on the question of moving the convertible
assets from Japan to the United States for safekeeping, bearing in mind
the probable political results of such action. In this regard, it is
suggested that a compromise, satisfactory to the banks, would be to
only move such portion of the convertible assets (approximately $40
million) as equal in value the amount of the cotton credit that will
probably be utilized during the four months subsequent to October 31,
1947, which is the date such utilization will begin. The movement of
this amount would seem politically justifiable on the grounds that
it is required to secure a commodity most essential to the revival of
the Japanese peace economy.

: OFD - Mr. Ness

October 7, 1947
M. SALTTMAN
A-S - Mr. Saltzman
FROM : OE - Mr. Whitman
A.5 fier
SUBJECT: Recent Developments in Program for Securing Bank
ecs
Credits to Finance Japanese Imports.
131148
1. Reference is made to my memorandum of September 17, 1947 on
"Arrangements for Securing Bank Credits to Finance Japanese Imports",
which was submitted (to Mr. Havlik and Mr. Saltzman) for your considera-
tion. This memorandum will supplement the previous memorandum, and bring
you up to date on more recent developments concerning this problem.
2. SCAP has been queried by the War Department on the question of
removing the gold and other convertible assets from Japan for safekeeping.
In reply, he recommended against transporting the gold, mainly on grounds
84/1151
of the political repercussions in Japan and in other FEC countries, of
such action. He stated however, that in his view, these considerations do
not apply to the silver which amounts to about one-third of the total, and
†he indorsed the War Department proposal to attempt, if necessary to secure
the bank credit, to sell the silver to the United States Treasury.
Gen. Draper remarked at a recent SVNCC meeting following his return from
Japan, that in informal discussions in Tokyo, he had been given to under-
stand that while SCAP did not favor the transport of the gold if it could
be avoided, he would not stand out against this measure if adequate bank
credits could not otherwise be secured.
3. A legal opinion from SCAP on the powers of the Supreme Commander
in connection with the utilization of Japanese-owned assets for trade pur-
poses, has been received and discussed with officials of the Export-Import
Bank. While this opinion is deemed to adequately set forth SCAP's authority
as a military occupier of Japan to take possession of and utilize publicly-
owned Japanese gold and silver, the Export-Import Bank officials are not yet
satisfied that the Far Eastern Commission policy statement provides all
19
necessary authority for the use, and if necessary, the dissipation of the
assets for trade purposes, rather than to hold the value thereof for
reparations. The Office of the Legal Adviser is furnishing the Export-
Import Bank with additional facts regarding the basis of authority of the
Far Eastern Commission, and the implementing power of SCAP in relation to
Far Eastern Commission policies.
4. An additional development which would appear to materially assist
in securing bank credits based on these assets, was the approval by Top
SWNCC on October 3, 1947 of SWNCC 236/49. This paper establishes as U.S.
policy that the Japanese trade deficit shall be a first charge against
certain categories of assets, including categories to be the initial assets
of the proposed import-export fund, and subject to the condition that
private credits extended for the procurement of Japanese imports will be
prior to the repayment of other advances (U.S. Government) for this pur-
pose. It would appear that this, together with the provision in the
Basic

=== Page 435 === (confidence 0.96)
-2 -
Basic Post-Surrender Policy for Japan, approved by the Far Eastern Com-
mission, which states that reparations shall not prejudice the defraying
of the costs of the occupation and the maintenance of a minimum standard
of living, provides all necessary support for our interpretation of the
Far Eastern Commission policy on utilization of these convertible assets
for trade purposes.
5. Representatives of the private banks concerned with this credit,
have raised certain additional legal points regarding the authority of
the controller of the export-import fund to safeguard their collateral,
in view of the fact that SCAP as such is not to be a party to loan con-
tracts. Means of satisfying the banks on this point are being diseussed
between SCAP and the Department of the Army.
6. The recommendations contained in paragraph 10 of the previous
memorandum are not considered to be altered by the facts cited above.

NOARD FORM NO. 64
DATE: November 3, 1947
TO
: CE - Mr. Whitman
sUBJECT: Liquidation Program
DRF is apprently talking only of stocks (20 to 25 billion total),
whereas the 50 percent estimata by SCAP may relate to total securities
(stocks, debentures, bonds, etc.). For the closed institutions, accord-
ing to SCAP figres, stocks were 2.4 (book value at closing date), bonds
(including governments) 11.3, and total assets 132.2. DRF has a figure
3.2 for shares of closed institutions. There may be a certain amount of
double counting, but this would also te true of total security figures
on which percentages are based.
On the problem posed by Reinstein, we have to break it down between
necessary liquidation under past anu irrevocable action and future action
regarding which we have discretion or the extent of which will depend
on administration of existing or proposed legislation. The liquidation
of assets of closed institutions, the proceeds of the capital levy and
the liquidation resulting from the Reorganization and Reconstruction
law, are necessary steps in the implementation of previous legislation
having a wider objective than simply liquidation, and the only questioh
is how and when the liquidation is to be accomplished. I feel the main
thing on these is speed, and would favor maximum independence of action
by the Japanese Government. SCAF has never made up his mind or been told
how much "supervision" by his staff is necessary, and thus I think it
594.51
has been poorly applied as shown in Roger's comments. This is what I
had in mind for soma sort of mission.
On the Zaibatsu program, I am not sure I know just where we stand.
I gather the security holdings of the top holding companies (list seems
to run about 67 companies with holdings of 6.2 shares and .5 other
securities)have been turned over to HCIc for liquidation. I don't know
exactly what portion of these are pure holding companies, but gather
from the list that all except 8 to 12 are holding-operating companies.
九
In addition, personal holdings of Zaibatsu "men" outside the top hold-
ing companies amount to .6. The necessity for liquidation of these
securities is also presumably not open to question.
Just how much the two measures to accomplish the remainder of the
program will or should add to the problem, seems to be the only open
question. These measures are the "Anti-Monopoly Law" passed by the
Diet last April, and the Deconcentration Law now approved by the Cabinet
and before the Diet. The Criental Economist estimates securities to be
liquidated under Anti-Monopoly Law at 12 billion, including about 90,000
different stocks. There may be overlapping between this figure and the
6.7 billion of the "Zaibatsu" group, although this is not self-evident.
How much the Deconcentration bill will add to this is anyone's guess.
The

=== Page 437 === (confidence 0.98)
-2 -
The total I get out of this is about 35-38 billion of stocks plus
11.2 bonds for the closed institutions, and an unknown number of bonds
resulting from capital levy receipts, R & R law and Zaibatsu program.
(I suppose top holding companies which are being dissolved as distinct
from reorganized held bonds and debentures, as well as stocks).
I don't know what conversations you have had with IR on this
subject, but I would like a discussion on the relation between the Anti-
Monopoly Law and Deconcentration bill, and how they are to be adminis-
tered. I assume the former is directed primarily against "practices"
and the latter against "institutions", but from my reading they seem
to overlap. The vague wording of the latter seems to me to make its
consequences for the Japanese economy entirely unpredictable. A
secondary question is the relation of these two measures to United Nations'
investments. Welsh apparently told Fullam (ITT) it would not apply to
foreign corporate holdings, but that is certainly far from clear from
its text. I suggest we talk about these points with IR at an early staff
meeting.

SCAP ANNOUNCES INTIRIM CURRENCY AIRANGLIFNT
TO FACILITATE TRADE VITH STLRLING AFFAS
Acoption of en interin currency arrangcrent by SCAP
and the Unitcd Kingcom to facilitate privatc trade between sterling
are as and Japan, was anncunced today by General MccArthur.
The arrangement provides that sterling will be accepted in
private trade for Japinesc goods and that SCAP, in turn, will pay
sterling for goods which are purchascd from sterling areas
cesignated by the United Kingdon. All sterling received by SCAP
will be acposited in tvwo accounts to be kept in London, and nll
outpaynents by SCAP vill be made fror these accounts. The sterling
brlances in thcs accounts will be converted to United States
collars semi-annually at the requcst of SCAP. The two central
accounts will be with the Hong Kong Shanghai Banking Corporation
nd the Chartered Bank of Indic, Australic, and China.
Porticipants in the interim agrcement so far include the United
Kingdon, her colonics and mandated and protected areas and the
Cominions of Australia and New Zealand. The other Cominions
and Hong Kong are not parties to the agrcement at this time.
While characterizing the arrangenent as a "substantial
cchievement" tovard liberalizing the terms of privote trade
between Japan end sterling areas, S. J. Janow, chief of the
Plans and Programs Group of thc Foreign Trade Division, explained
that it is merely e prinary m asure intended to allow British
traders to buy more freely in Jopan until an overall currency
greement is reached. Negotiations on an ovcrall agreement have
been in progress in Woshington D. C. for some time.
The currency probler, Mr. Janow noted, has been a major ob-

GENERAL HEADQUARTERS
SUPRELE COMMANDER FOR THE ALLIED POWERS
APO 500
AG 091.31 (18 Dec 47)ESS/FT
18 December 1947
MEMORANDUM FOR: United Kingdom Liaison Mission in Japan
SUBJECT:
Interim Agreement for Financing Private
Trade with Japan
1. Reference is United Kingdom Liaison Mission memorandum
number 94/301/47, dated 28 November 1947, on the subject of
financing private trade with Japan.
2. Reference paragraph 2 of the above memorandum. Prices
quoted in dollars should be converted into sterling at the rate
of 4.03, the rate registered with the International Monetary Fund.
This practice will be followed in connection with sales to the
sterling area.
3. Reference paragraph 3 of the above memorandum. It appears
reasonable that the rate applied to the conversion of sterling
should be formalized in a manner identical with that applied to
sales, namely, the rate registered with the International Monetary
Fund. In this connection it is understood that at the end of six
months after the coming into effect of the original agreement, con-
version of sterling into dollars may be accomplished at the option
of either party to the agreement upon call therefor. Conversion is
to take place at the rate registered with the International Monetary
Fund at the time of conversion.
4. Reference paragraph 4 of the above memorandum. This confirms
the readiness of the Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers to
convert into dollars at the end of six months any sterling proceeds
of the account which may then stand to the credit of the sterling
area.
FOR THE SUPREE COMLANDER:
R. M. LEVY
Colonel, AGD
Adjutant General

MEMORANDUM FOR: J.PNESE GOVERNMENT
T.LROUGH:
Central Liaison Office, Tokyo
SUBJLCT:
Call for Payment of Unpaid Capital Stock of All
Financial Institutions in Japan
1. The Japanese Government is directed to issue immediately
a call for the payment in full of all unpaid capital stock of all
financial institutionsin Japan including the Bank of Japan and
insurance companies. Aotual payment by the Bank of Japan may be
suspended until an appropriation of funds has been made by the
Diet. actual payment by a Speclal accounting Oompany will be sus-
pended until the approval of the reorganization plan, and will be
paid within thirty days thereafter.
2. The Japanese Government is direoted to complete this
call within ninety days from the date of this Memorandum. Failure
to meet the call will cause the owners of such stock to lose all
right, title, interests and benefits pertaining thereto.
3. Notwithstanding any provisions of the Financial Institu-
tions Reconstruction and Reorganization Law, the foregoing
procedures will be applicable to owners as of the date hereof,
and failure to meet the call will not relieve the liability of
owners of record as of 1l august 1946, and owners between that
date and 26 November 1947. Owners who acquire the stock on or
after the date of this Memorandum will likevise assume liability
for payment of the unpaid portion.
4. Paragraphs 2 and 3 will not be applicable to:
a. Closed institutions
b. United Nations Nationals
c. Other non-Japanese Nationals residing outside of Japan.
5. In order to implement the provisions of this memorandum,
direct communicatin betwee. the ministry of Finance and interested
staff sections of General Headquarters, Supreme Commander for the
allied Powers, is hereby authorized.
FOR THE SUPRNE COMMLANDER:

FOR JAPAN

CONFIDENTIAL (For Department Use Only)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
Subject:
: Economic Rehabilitation Occupied Areas (EROA) Estimate
i5
for Japan.
RECEIVED
894.51/2-1048
18
A
The Honorable
The ¿Secretary of State,
Washington.
11
Sir:
12
I have the honor to refer to the pending Economic Rehabilita-
tion Occupied Areas (EROA) estimate for Japan and to submit the
following comments of this Mission.
The EROA estimate for Japan for Fiscal Year 1948-1949 (15-month
period beginning April 1, 1948) amounts to $452,278,903, of which an
estimated 272,228,490 is to be financed through the medium of the
Occupied Japan Export-Import Revolving Fund (OJEIRF), available credits,
or barter on open account, and an estimated $180,050,413 is to be re-
quested of Congress. It is the understanding of this Mission that the
"recovery" or "rehabilitation program" as outlined above was prepared
in the Civil Affairs Division of the Department of the Army with thei
AS
assistance of representatives of General Headquarters of the Supreme
Commander for the Allied Powers and was predicated in part upon the
findings set forth in Possibility of a Balanced Japanese Economy pre
pared by the Research and Statistics Division, of the Economic and
Scientific Section of General Headquarters and transmitted to the
Department by this Mission's despatch No. 27/of January 9, 1948, en-
titled "Transmittal of a Report by the Economic and Scientific Section,
General Headquarters, Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers, Concern-
ing the Possibility of Achieving a Balanced Japanese Economy." The
CSN
program as drafted indicates that, if funds in the approximate amount
referred to above are appropriated for the purpose of purchasing basic
commodities needed for Japan's industrial machine (Category II items)
as distinguished from purchases made with funds appropriated for Govern-
ment Aid and Relief in Occupied Areas (GARIOA) in order to maintain a
"disease and unrest" level (Category I items), the result ant stimulus
to Japan's economy will pennit the procurement of such basic commodities
through non-appropriated funding media, and will obviate the necessity
of appropriating funds in the future for rehabilitation purposes. This
stimulus to Japan's industrial machine in tum, according to the proposed
program, will reduce requirements for "diseaso and unrest" or Category I
appropriations to the extent that equilibrium will have been restored to
Japants balance of trade by the calendar year 1953.
Apart from the question of whether or not an appropriation approx
imating $180,000,000 might prove to be the determining factor in achieving
a possible balance of trade by 1953, it would seem that consideration should
be given to the attitude of other governments, particularly those in theo
Far East, toward such a rehabilitation program, and the possible undesirt
ability of linking an appropriation request to a balance of trade
projection
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 465 === (confidence 0.99)
CONFIDENTIAL (For Department Use Only)
Tokyo1s No. 82,
February 10, 1948.
-2-
projection in the event equilibrium is not restored in accordance
with the proposed timetable, particularly in view of the relative-
ly small amount involved in relation to Japan's economic condition.
With respect to the former consideration, there are enclosed as
examples of foreign criticism to the rehabilitation program, two
editorial comments taken from the Chinese Press Review for Jan-
1/2/
uary 28 and 29, 1948, prepared by the American Consulate General at
Shanghai, China. As might be imagined, the reaction indicated in
the edit orial comments is unfavorable, the theme being that the
United States intends to rehabilitate the Japanese economy in order
to open the Japanese market to American business and to provide a
check-point against Soviet expansion. One writer predicts a boycott
of Japanese goods by other Asiatic countries and that action taken
by the United States to assist Japan to regain its pre-war preeminent
industrial position in the Far East will only increase pro-Soviet
sentiment in Asia.
Assuming that approximately the same amount is to be financed
through OJEIRF, available credits, and barter, as indicated in the
EROA proposal, it is the opinion of this Mission, unless there are
overriding considerations unknown to us here, that possibly the
same objective in terms of funding could have been secured by
approving an increased GARIOA estimate rather than seeking Con-
gressional approval for a labelled "rehabilitation" program in the
amount of approximately $180,000,000. It is interesting to note
that the total of both the 1949 Fiscal Year Government Aid and
Relief in Occupied Areas (GARIOA) estimate for Japan of approximately
$435,000,000 (estimate for Japan and the Ryukyus $468,955,560), as
approved by the Department of Army, and the proposed $180,050,413
EROA appropriation request, is less than the original SCAP GARIOA
estimate of $684,753,054 (subsequently revised in August 1947 to
$685,850,876) for Category I, or, "disease and unrest" requirements.
Of those it ems included in the EROA estimate, $3,000,000 have
been earmarked under project 500 for the purchase of machinery,
motors, parts, et cetera, and $10,000,000 under project 540 for the
purchase of incentive goods and war surplus equipment. It is the
understanding of this Mission that these items would normally not
be considered Category I items for which GARIOA funds could be ex-
pended and hence, to provide funds in this regard, a Congressional
appropriation such as the proposed EROA, including such Category II
items, would be required, or, financing through the medium of OJEIRF,
available credits or barter. On the other hand, this Mission is of
the opinion that the 31,536,000 estimated for project 550-5,000,000
to be financed through OJEIRF or other media and $26,536,000 through
Congressional appropriation, to be used for the purchase of agri-
cultural supplies, namely, livestock feed, ammonium nitrate, and
phosphate rock-might possibly have been included as a GARIOA, or
Category I item. The original GARIOA estimate as submitted by SCAP
included a $3,825,000 item for phos phate rock and $20,400,000 for
livestock feed; however, these amounts were deleted by the Department
of the Army. The same conclusion might be reached with regard to a
substantial portion of the $58,300,000 estimated for project 560—
$23,400,000 to be financed through OJEIRF or other media, and
$34,900,000 through Congressional appropriation--to be used for the
transportation of civilian supplies. SCAP's original GARIOA estimate
for the transportation of civilian supplies amounted to $141,517,000;
however, this item was reduced to $85,566,019 by the Department of the
Army.
The
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 466 === (confidence 0.99)
CONFIDENTIAL (For Department Use Only)
Tokyo's No. 82,
February 10, 1948.
一3-
The core of the proposed $452,278,903 EROA estimate is the
$349,442,903 estimated for project 530—$243,828,490 to be finaneed
through OJEIRF or other media, and $105,614,413 through Congression
al appropriation--to be used for the purchase of basic commodities.
In turn, of this total figure, Japan's textile fiber requirements,
estimated at $231,000,000--$214,056,000 to be financed through non
appropriated funds and $16,944,000 through Congressional appropria-
tion--constitute the major item. We believe that Japan's estimated
textile requirement s could possibly be met through GARIOA appropria-
tion as well as OJEIRF and other non-appropriated funds. In this
connection, it is interesting to note that SCAP's Fiscal Year 1949
GARIOA estimate, as originally submitted, included a textile fibers
requirement in the amount of $120,861,842 ($97,406,342 cotton) to
prevent "widespread disease and unrest". This estimate was predicated
upon a basic consumer ration of 2-1/2 pounds of textile fibers per
capita (77.98 per cent of ration cotton) and upon the assumption that
the only textile stocks available for household use would have to be
met from new production. This item was deleted from SCAP's GARIOA
estimate as being too difficult to justify, and it is presently
planned, as indicated in the above EROA estimate to meet the bulk
of Japan's textile requirements through other than appropriated fund
sources. The EROA estimate, as contrasted with the original GARIOA
estimate, provides for an estimated two pounds of cotton per capita
for domestic clothing use. By absorbing domestic clothing needs
under GARIOA, the quantity required to be financed through OJEIRF and
other media would be decreased thereby. Non-appropriated funds could
be directed in part to the purchase of that portion of Japan's raw
cotton requirements permitted to be procured from foreign sources,
whereas the GARIOA appropriation could be utilized in the procurement
of the stipulated share of Japan's raw cotton requirements secured to
the United States. By so doing, this Mission is of the opinion that
the $88,670,413 EROA estimate to be requested from Congress for the
purchase of raw materials such as metals and metallic ores, minerals,
chemicals, coal, pulp, et cetera, could be financed through the medium
of OJEIRF, available credits and barter.
As has been indicated above, no attempt has been made to discuss
whether or not a rehabilitation appropriation in the approximate
amount of $180,000,000 will so stimulate Japan's economy as to result
in a balanced foreign trade by 1953. Rather, the attention of the
Department has been invited to the desirability of achieving the
objectives of the EROA program in its present form through some other
medium more palatable perhaps to foreign governments and less diffi-
cult to justify by our own. It is believed that this approach to the
"rehabilitation program" merits some consideration in view of the
amount involved and the possibility of includ ing a part of the items
indicated within a GARIOA appropriation and financing the balance
through non-appropriated means, the difficulties involved in securing
Congressional approval for a "rehabilitation program" labelled as such,
criticism of such a program by other countries, particularly those in
the Far East, and the somewhat tenuous position taken that the injec-
tion of approximately $180,000,000 into Japan's distraught economy may
result in a trade balance by 1953. As a consequence, this Mission is
of the opinion that a more desirable course of action might have been
to increase the GARIOA estimate to approximately the original figure as
submitted, utilize OJEIRF, barter, and other available credits as
planned, and not submit the proposed EROA request in its present form
to
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 467 === (confidence 0.98)
CONFIDENTIAL (Department Use Only)
Tokyots No. 82,
February 10, 1948.
-4-
to Congress. On the other hand, if a largescale rehabilitation
program is contemplated--one that is thought sufficiently sound
to risk unfavorable foreign criticism and Congressional diffioul-
ties, then it would seem that resort to a separate Congressional
rehabilitation appropriation request would be necessary.
This Mission realizes that the EROA program for Japan has tak
definite shape and is at present under consideration by the appro
priate individuals and agencies in Washington. It is believed, how
ever, that the above comments, predicated upon information available
to this Mission, are of possible interest to the Department, parti-
cularly in regard to the international aspects of the program as well.
as for reference should a similar situation arise in future requests for
appropriations relating to the economic rehabilitation of Japan.
Respectfully yours,
W.Be.
W. J. Sebald
Acting Political Adviser

EDITORIAL COMMENT
(COPY)
We Oppose the U.S. Plan for Rehabilitating
the Japanese Economy
Ta Kung Pao (Independent, Political Science Group), Shanghai
January 28, 1948
The United States has worked out a naked and concrete plan for
helping Japan to rehabilitate her economy. The memorandum on this
plan has already been submitted to the Far Eastern Commission for
examination and has also been sent to the Chinese Ministry of
Foreign Affairs.
Although the contents of the plan have not yet been made public
by official quarters, yet judging by the fragmentary materials which
are already available, it is not difficult for us to see a general
outline of the plan. This is a big plan for economic rehabilitation;
it is a six or five year plan for reconstruction. Beginning from
July this year, the United States will lend US$500 million or US$650
million to Japan, and in addition, she will make free investments in
Japan totalling some US$2,O00 million. When the plan is completely
carried out, Japan will be restored to the industrial level which
existed during the period 1930-1934 (actually, this level will be
exceeded), and according to the plan, during the last year Japan will
export a tremendous quantity of commodities with a total value of
US$1,400 million.
Now on the basis of the above material we wish to point out
what the United States hopes to achieve. First, the large amount of
money required by the plan will, of course, be invested by the
United States. The latter wants to control Japan's industries by
exporting her capital, so that Japan's economic life will depend
entirely upon her. In this way, Japan will become the 49th State of
the U.S.A. The United States wants to include Japan in the Dollar
Bloc, and then to use her as a strategic base. Fundamentally, this
is a way of reviving Japan's traditional policy of having an
industrialized Japen and an agricultural Asia; it will enable the
"Greater East Asia Coprosperity Sphere" to revive in another form.
Naturally, the British bloc will be able to share in the advantages
of this plan, for with Indian cotton and Australian wool, it will
have capital with which to cooperate with Japan.
Secondly, the central purpose of the plan is to murture Japan's
iron and steel industry, so as to enable it to produce 3 million
tons of iron and steel annually, and also to develop her textile
industry and to develop and grab th: Asiatic market. At the same
time, the plan calls for the development of the chemical fertiliser
industry in Japan. Regarding these three industries, textiles are
a weapon to be used in the peacetime struggle, while iron and steel
and fertiliser are war materials. The American plan is very care-
fully worked out, for it is designed to enable Japan to dominate East
Asia both in peace-time and war-time. The objective is to enable
Japan to achieve domination rather than to solve the problem of the
livelihood of the Japanese people.
Thirdly,
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 469 === (confidence 0.99)
CONFIDENTIAL
Enclosure No. 1 to Tokyo's No. 82
dated February 10, 1948.
-2-
Thirdly, the economic significance of the industrial level
which the plan is designed to achieve is to bring about an unbalanced
development of the Japanese economy, so that Japan will have to de-
pend upon the United States or the Anglo-American bloc for raw
materials and food supplies and will not become self-sufficient,
thus opening the Japanese market for American capitalists.
Fourthly, the different Asiatic countries will be placed under an
industrialized Japan. First of all, economic pressure will be
brought to bear upon these countries. An extremely sharp weapon in
the form of Japanese goods will be used against us for the purpose of
destroying the defense works of the industries of the different Asiatic
countries. This will then be followed by political and military
pressure. The industries of the different Asiatic countries, especially
those of China, will be unable to compete successfully with the Japan-
ese industries, which are supported by America's huge capital, so
that we shall never be able to raise our heads again.
Moreover, we should point out, solemnly point out, that this
policy of the United States towards Japan is self-contradictory, be-
cause according to the principles of the development of Capitalism,
Japan will doubtless eventually ask the United States for independence
for nationalistic reasons. What Japan lacks now is nothing but
capital, machinery and high-grade technical ability, so by the time
the United States has supplied Japan with enough of these things, the
Japanese ruling classes will want the United States to get out of
their country. Next, the United States feels that it will be worth-
while to seek control of the general situation in Asia by aiding
Japan, because it will require only a small capital but will yield
good profits. She is mistaken! Although Japan has had experience
in conquering or trying to conquer various Asiatic countries, yet the
United States should realize that in giving all-out aid to Japan, she
will be thoroughly antagonizing the people of other Asiatic countries.
Even if the Japanese goods bear U.S. trade marks, the people of other
Asiatic countries, we are sure, will launch a boycott movement against
such goods--a boycott which will. include U. S. goods as well. There-
fore, the Asiatic market for Japanese goods now envisaged by the
United States will be merely a nightmare and will be non-existent.
Even if Japan will be used only to check Soviet expansion, it will
only cause us to charge our hatred for Japan to the United States'
account and will, we are afraid, only make the different Asiatic
countries more pro-Soviet.
There is yet another reason why we oppose the United States'
plan for rehabilitating the Japanese economy, and that is, that since
the Japanese peace treaty has not yet been signed, Japan is still an
enemy of the Allied nations. As one of the Allied nations, the
United States is now so openly helping Japan, which is an enemy nation,
to recover. This has no moral and treaty basis whatsoever. Under no
circumstances must China endorse the United States' plan; she must
flatly reject any pleasing words or favors, and must strongly oppose
this plan. (Summary)
DMBane/hp

EDITORIAL COMMENT
We Refute the View That Japan Should Be Made
the Factory of Asia
Tung Nan Jih Pao (KMT Southeast China Organ), Shanghai
January 29.1948
The United States' plan for rehabilitating Japan's industry and
economy has already been formally submitted to the Far Eastern Commission
and to China. While the contents of the plan have not yet been made
public, yet from the fact that the United States is ready to extend a
US$500 million loan to Japan and that Japan is now drafting a five-year
plan, we can see that the plan for rehabilitating Japan as proposed by
the United States indicates that the latter is determined to convert
Japan into a so-called "factory of Asia", just as she is trying to convert
Germany into a "factory of Europe". This is a matter of very great
significance.
Now, leaving aside the U.S. plan for rehabilitating Japan, let us
discuss the political significance of this plan first. The conclusion
reached by us is that China should strongly oppose this plan.
Generally speaking, the plan for rehabilitating Japan which the
United States has proposed has the same aim as her plan for rehabilitating
Germany, viz: on the one hand, she wants to consider the rehabilitation
of Japan as the backbone of the rehabilitation of the Far Eastern economy,
and on the other hand, she wants to use the rehabilitation of Japan as
the keystone in strengthening her anti-Soviet strategic bases. However,
we are more suspicious and uneasy about the plan for the rehabilitation
of Japan than are the people of Western Europe about the plan for the
rehabilitation of Germany. This is because according to the "five-year
plan" which Japan has drafted, the rehabilitation of that country will
be very beneficial to the United States alone (the British Empire can
also benefit from it to a certain extent), but will do great harm to
China and the other Asiatic countries. In carrying out the plan for the
rehabilitation of Japan, the United States may be able to convert that
country into a 49th State of the U.S.A., but at the same time, Japan
will be able to include the Asiatic countries in the "Great East Asia
Coprosperity Sphere" which she will rebuild. What a picture would that
be?
Any plan that is designed to convert Japan into the "factory of
Asia" again will not be tolerated by the Asiatic countries. History
must not be allowed to go backward. Today, most of the Asiatic countries
do not have a right to voice their views independently, and China is
the only Asiatic nation which has attained a high international position,
so we should express our views on this matter on behald of our sister
nations in Asia as well as ourselves. (Summary)
DMBane/bo

STANDARD FORM NO. 64
TO : Mr. Ness - OFD
DATE: March 10, 1948
FROM : Mr. Whitman - OE
sUBJECT: Proposed Cotton Credit for Japan.
1. Principal Features of Credit.
SCAP, through the CAD Division, Department of the Army,
and in partial coordination with the Department of State, has
been negotiating for a $60 million credit to purchase American
raw cotton for Japan. Present plans contemplated a $29 million
participation by the Export-Import Bank in this credit, remainder
to be taken up by consortium of private banks. Interest on notes
running for 10 months (maximum period of the credit) will be 3≈
with lesser rates for shorter period paper. Repayment is expected
to be primarily from textile exports supplemented by other Japan-
ese dollar exports, with the total credit secured on a one-to-one
basis by Japanese-owmed :precious metals made available for this
purpose by a policy decision of the FEC. Obligor under the credit
and administrator of the Revolving Fund (from which repayment will
894.51
be made and in which security is held) will be a controller
appointed by SCAP.
2. Interest of the Department of State.
The Department has prepared and is supporting a plan for
the economic recovery of Japan to be accomplished mainly by an
expansion of Japanese foreign trade. It is considered that ade-
quate financing of raw cotton imports is an essential feature of
w
the above-mentioned program as cotton textile exports have amounted
to about 60% of total Japanese exports during the occupation. The
-1048
absence of an explicit dollar sales requirement in this credit
should permit greater flexibility in the sale of textiles than
has heretofore been possible. The Department considers that all
responsibilities of SCAP to FEC in connection with the use of the
gold and silver have been met.
3. Controversial Features of the Credit.
a. Limits to Borrowing and the Rate of Interest. The
one-to-one ratio of borrowings to liquid security (precious metals
and cash) permitted in the agreement combined with the 32% interest
rate (plus fees) appears rather onerous. Offsetting considera-
tions are 1) the pressing need of SCAP for the cotton if produc-
tion is to be maintained, 2) physical location of the gold in
RM/R
Japan
nal-
'ev_
at

=== Page 477 === (confidence 0.98)
-2 -
Japan and political difficulties of moving it except in emergency
or in event of forced liquidation, 3) lack of experience of the
borrower in financial and marketing aspects of the transaction,
4) apparent tightening of bank credit in general.
b. Why Borrow rather than Liquidate the Security? The
political considerations involved in using these assets that FEC
has designated for ultimate disposition as reparations, has prompted
the Department to support the proposed procedure. It is recognized
that some liquidation to repay the credits may eventually be
necessary unless dollar markets for Japanese textiles improve. This
latter consideration also gives some added justification to the
bankers insistence on one-to-one security.
4. Recommendation.
That the Department support favorable early action on the
matter by the Export-Import Bank and the NAC.

It will be noted that the agreement is broad
and generalized in scope and wording, as might be
expected from the coalition from which it has arisen.
The very fact that an agreement has been reached, how-
ever, is of significance from a practical point of view,
because it has allowed Prime Minister ASHIDA to form
his cabinet. Most of the controversial issues are
policies advocated by the Social-Democratic Party,
especially by the left wing faction of that party, and
are to be resolved later in some manner, mainly by
committees.

definite course of action has beer postponed:

nationalization of key industries are likely to be

Dear Mr. Secretary:
page 3) that Japan is to receive a combined government-private bank
credit of $60 million to begin with and rising to $120 million
subsequently. Fifty percent of the original amount is to be extended
by National City, Dhase, and Bank of America and the rest is to be
taken'by the Export-Import Bank. The loan, which is to cover the
purchase of Ameriean cotton, will be secured by gold and certain
other securities held by Japan in the amount of $l20 million.
We have heard that the rate on this piece of business is very
satisfactory and we are wondering why the deal is consummated with
these very few great banks. Shouldn't smaller banks be permitted
to subscribe to this loan or that the big banks farm out part of
these loans to their correspondent banks? Perhaps in the future the
smaller banks could be circularized to find out whether they would
like to participate in an operation of this kind. Perhaps the Export-
Import Bank could circularize the smaller banks through The American
Bankers Association.
If we are to maintain a truly capitalistic system, the government
must see to it that the small banks have the same privileges to
participate in these government-arranged good loans as do the larger
banks. A democratio economy is essential to a democracy.
FILED
Yours very truly,

I have the honor to transmit herewith current information
with regard to progress in sales of securities by the respective
Japanese governmental Liquidation agencies, namely the Closed
Institutions Liquidating Commission (CILC), the Holding Company
Liquidation Commission (HCIc), and the Securities Coordinating
Liquidation Committee (SCLC).
The CIIC was organised for the purpose of liquidating the
assets of those concerns (principally certain control asseciations
and firms holding overseas assets) nominated for closure by the
Antitrust and Cartels Division of the Reonomic and Seientific
Sedtion of this Headquarters. The HCLC was organized for the pur-
pose of liquidating certain designated holding companies (as in
number) as well as the properties of certain individuals desig-
nated as holding exmessive economic control (54 in number); in
addition, sales of shares resulting from the reorganisation of
those companies desigaated as excessive sconomic consentrations
under the provisions of the Economio Deconcentration Law are to
be handled by this ageney. The SCLC was erganised to coordinate
the sales of securities made by the respective agensies concerned
with liquidation processes and to determine, for example, the men-
ner, time and place of sales, as well as price levels; but the
activities of this committee to date would indicate that it has

assmed functions in exmess of those narmally perforned by a co-
ordinating ageney.

Stocks still held by the Japanese Ministry of Finanee, SCLC,

This does not take into account, however, the substantial but as

value of other types of securities, ineluding bends and debentures,

Thus far, securities have been sold by competitive bid (this
type of sale is usually handled by SCLC), negotiated sale, amd by
sale to the public at a fixed priee (the latter two types of sale
are usually handled by HCLC, CILC, and Bank of Japan). With res-
peet to competitive bid sales (approximately one-half of the shares
sold thus far have been disposed of by this methed), the offering
is usually made by public tender which is accomplished by pablish-
ing a list of securities offered for sale in newspapers in those
centers where the offering is to be made (seeurities allecated to
principal centers on a population basis) for a period of one week
as well as by posting sueh a list in the principal offices of the
SCIC for a similar period. Bids are opened twe days after the
expiration of the one week period, and ascppted in order beginning
with the highest. As a rule, bids are not accepted at a priee
below a predetermined level. Although this type sale is open to
the general public, exparience has indicated that dealers and bro-
kers are usually the purchasers, and as a consequence SCLC mmst await
their reports to ascertain the ultimate buyers of seeurities sold
by public tender. In this connection, the persons concemed with
such sales in the Antitrust and Cartels Division admit that it is
impossible to maintain any degree of effective control over the
ultimate disposition of stock shares sold in this fashion.
There are sales of a somewhat different nature made to employ-
ces of the issuing company and to persons residing in the comumity
in which the issuing company's principal plant is losated. These

of sesurities by individuals, many of whom have obtained yen funds
illegally and whe wish te exohange them for securities. In se far
as this Mission can determine, present methods of stock dispesal do
not eliminate the ultimate possibility that a relatively small group
of individuals may acquire ence again what may be deemed exsessive
economic control in terms of owmership of enterprises. The persoms
concerned in the above-named Divisien do not deny this pessibility,
but take the position that their objective will have been achieved
if a wider distribution of steck owmership is effected than existed
befere the war. In the absence of effective measures taken te con-
trol the ultimate disposition of stock shares, this Mission is of
the opinion that the securities liquidation program may result, in
part at least, in a transfer of esenomie omership from the hands of
one group, the Zaibatsu in the main, to another, primarily somprising
those persons who acquired yen wealth (in mapy instances illegally--
the "new yen" class) during the post-war era.

DEPARTMENT OF THE ARMY
WASHINGTON, D. C.
MAA31948
DEP OF STATE
Honorable George C. Marshall
271948
Secretary of State
OIVISION OF FANACIAL AFFARS
Dear Mr. Marshall:
In accordance with the action taken by the National
Advisory Council in connection with the proposed Japanese
cotton credit, negotiations were carried on yesterday with
894.51/4-2148
(OR)
representatives of the commercial banks. I have discussed
the matter previously with Governor Szymczak of the Federal
Reserve Board and Mr. Southard of the Treasury Department
us
inbrder to get their best advice.
The banks proposal, in effect, was for a 32% interest
rate and payment by SCAP of $50,000 in attorney's fees and
other expenses. It was evident that the banks would not
reduce the 3 interest rate under the conditions proposed
but the final outcome was a 3% rate with the banks' agree-
ment to reduce the rate to 22% until maturity if it should
later devetop that the so-called "gold pot" were transferred
into the United States.
It was also agreed that SCAP would not pay the $50,000
legal fees and expenses, but that if the credit was not
used once to the extent of half of the total, namely,
$30,000,000, then SCAP would pay half of the actual fees and
expenses, but with a ceiling of $25,000.
DEPARTMENI
It is believed that this was the best arrangement under
the circumstances that could be effected to make the credit
ECONOMIC AFFAIRS
available to SCAP promptly, in accordance with the program
which both SCAP and the Department of the Army consider
important.
NTERNATIONAL RESOUNCES OIVISION
Wreeian H. Ahraper
Sincerely yours,
MAY 3 1948
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
William H. Draper Jr.
Under Secretary of the Army

Ma. Bare
This highly significant despatch on the sale of securities
through Liquidation Agencies reaches fwo important conclusions:
1) that as of March, 1948, only about 4% of the shares of liquidated
holding companies and properties heve been resold, and that none
of their bonds and debentures have been resold; 2) that in the
absence of effective measures to regulate the ultimate disposition
of theseshares, "the securities liquidation progrem may result,
in part at leest, in a transfer of economic ownership from the
hands of one group (mainly the Zaibatsu) to another, primarily
comprising those persons who acguired yen wealth (in many instances
illegally - the new yen' clase) during the post-war era."
According to Mr.. Bane, about 12 million shares of liquidated
companies, associations and properties had been sold as of March 20,
1948. About 250 million shares remained in the hands of the
Japanese Government (Ministry of Finance, the Bank of Japan and
the various liquidating agencies). At the same time, no sales of
00
bonds and debentures of these liquidated institutions had been
6
R
consummated; and there had, of course, been no sales of shares
of those companies designated under the Economic Decentralization
C
Law. Such is Mr. Bane's description of the delayed progress of
5
the deconcentration program. I do not think that SCAp Head-
1/4
quarters wouid take exception to any of the above assertions,
which are, after all, posited on the Heaaquarters tabulations
enclosed With Mr. Bane's despatch. However, Mr. Welch, Chief
1
of SCAP'e Anti-Trust and Cartels Division, pointed out to me that
2248
these securities are being disposed of at sn increasingly rapid
rate (this seems to be borne out by a cursory exemination of the
tabulations) wh ch will be further incressed as the price level
becomes more stebilized, as compeny reorgenizations approach com-
pletion, and upon the estaulishment of a stock exchange. But
even taking these end other arguments enumerated by Mr. Welch
into consideration, it seems improbable that all securities will
be disposed of within 20 months, es is claimed by SCAP.
Mr. Bare demonstrates that te most common method of dis-
posal of the securities is throun public tender and that although
this type of sale is open to the general public, experience has
shown that dealers end broxers are usually the curchasers. Even
the Anti-Trust and Cartels Division is said to edmit that no

5
of stock shares sold through these channels. According to

Denator Vandenberg:
Would you please tell me what money and why that has been

ask me why our Government is giving money to Japan? I do not know
I recently heard via radio that we were loaning Japan money
tngetker to get their textiles stated again. I am in favor of a
lagn to Japan as from what I saw they will repay it. ? I'm
definetely oppossed to this givings not only to Japan but to the
Changshi Sheck? Govt as no one over there has any good word for
the Chinese Govt.
And I am definately opposed to this Military Training program.
I think if in our schools there could be some form of training say in
High School - where respect would and could be taught- that is all
we need. Maybe it should be in our lower grades - say 6th and 7th
grade. We surely do not want to follow inthe foot-steps of the Foreig
Nations - and its time we the American pe/ople awakened to that
fact.
Now I'm speaking not only for myself but many people are wondering why
we Go and bomb countries or fight their war or them take our American
youth to save what??

Ars. Jedd King

as yor fonor)
. reat deal of money has been advanced by the
United States for the maintenance of tranquil condi-
tions in Japan during the occupation of that country.
These expenditures have accounted for large shipments
of soveral types of civilian goods, including food, from
the United States to Japan in order to prevent "disease
and unrest" wthich would otherwise make an orderly
advance toward democratization in Japan an impossibility.
This extra financial load on the United Statea is
necessary because the war left Japan in desperate strsits
as reg:rds the essentials of life for her orowded mil-
CS/A
lions. Humanitarian consideration for a prostrate
enemy and practical considerations of United States
interests in the Far Bast dictated these policies.
Thus, if there is to be an end to Japanese aggression
in Asia, wo must try to assure that Japan will become
a peace-loving demooratic nation; the best way to ob-
tain this result is to maintain an orderly and efficient
military occupation in Japan until the seeds of demooracy
are firmly planted. This goal involves a heavy burden
upon the American tax eyer, but the alternative of
modiately withdrawing our forces from Japan might be
upore costly in the long run.

w, In addition to the money we are providing
in order to provent 'disease and unrest' in Japan
which would be prejudioial to the attaining of the

that if Japan is provided with quantities of materials
such as American raw cotton, she can put back to work
mush of her idle labor and factories with the result
of producing greater quantities of goods for export.

to make it possible for Japan to pay for her essential
imports, and thus relieve the United States of this
burden. "ithout such economic recovery in Japan, the
imports or credits required from the United States
might go on indefinitely, with little prospeot of
repayment.

and democratic intentions of Japan are succeasful as
we all hope, the money expended for these purposes
will have been wisely spent. Then, at the ultimate
peace settlement with Japan we can examine various
other questions including Japan's over-all debt to the

=== Page 696 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure to Despatch No. 424 dated
July 8, 1948 from the Acting Political
Adviser for Japan, Tokyo, entitled
"Transmission of General Headquarters
Staff Memorandum Regarding Japanese
Government Financing".
UNCLASSIFIED
(COPY)
GENERAL HEADQUARTERS
SUPREME CONTANDER FOR THE ALLIED POWERS
AND
FAR EAST COMMAND
AG 319 (10 Jan 48)ESS
APO 500
18 February 1948
STAFF ELIORANDUM
.8
(SCAP & FEC)
JAPANESE GOVARN UT FINANCING
1. As a restraint on the current inflationary trend in Japan,
expenditures by the Japanese Government will be kept to the absolute
minimum ccnsistent with the objectives of the Occupation. Effective
implementation of this policy is essential to the ultimate stabilization
of the economy of Japan.
2. With the purpose of reducing yen expenditures to the minimum,
each staff section of General Headquarters, Supreme Commander for the
Allied Powers and Far East Command, will review present and proposed
Japanese Government revenues and expenditures on programs in which it
has primery interest. Availability of funds within budgeted amounts
under which the Japanese Government is operating will be considered
prior to final action on any new program.
3. The Economic and Scientific Section is assigned the primary
responsibility for the implementation of the provisions of this staff
memorandum. All actions by other staff sections affecting the expenditures
or reveuues of the Japanese Government will be referred to the Economic
and Scientific Section for concurrence prior to submission to the Chief
of Staff.
DY COMAND OF GENERAL LaCARTHUR:
PAUL J. MUELLER,
Major General, General Staff Corps,
Chief of Staff.
OFFICLAL:
/signed/
R. M. LEVY
Colonel, AGD,
Adjutant General.
UNCLASSIFIED

Dear Mr. Martin:
The purpose of this letter is to outline to you, in confirmatien
of informal convereations between representatives of thie Depertment
and your bank, the present situation with respect to requests reseived
recently by the Secretariat of the Far Eastern Commission frem member
nations of the Commission. This request is for the release to the
member FEC nations of copies of the Agreement of May 13 betwsen the 0
Occupied Japan Export-Import Revolving Fund and the Controller thereefO
on the one hand, and, on the other, a consortium of American banks, 4
headed by your institution.
5
The above mentioned request has been considered within the Depart-
ment of State, and conversations have also taken place thereon betwmen
Department of the Army, as well as with representatiwes of your bank. I
Mr. Sexton of your staff and Mr. Bachman of the Department of the AnmyO
are among those who have participated in these conversations. The4
resulting consensus is to the effect that, subject to a poliey docisio
by the Department of State, there is no objection by the interested
10
U.S. agencies to the distribution, by the Secretariat of the Far
Eastern Commission to member nations of the Commission, of the above
mentioned Agreement, and Exhibits, now printed in booklet form.
The document in question is, as you know, unclassified and is
based on FEC documents which have been fully distributed to the
member nations of the Commission. As a matter of policy, therefore,
it is the view of this Department that the above mentioned Agresment
should be made available to the member nations of the Commission.
Although I am informed that a large part of the information contained
in the Agreement has appeared in the press, all records of this
nature are issued to the member nations of the FEC under "restricted"
classification.

=== Page 709 === (confidence 0.98)
August 18, 1948
MEMORANDUM FOR THE FILE:
894.51/8-1848
Subject: Attached letter to Mr. Nelson Johnson,
Secretary General of the Far Eastern Commission
Mr. Frank Turner asked me whether the U. S. Government had
any objection to the distribution of the document referred to in
the attached letter. This document is not classified and a opy of
it was given to Mr. Turner by the Civil Affairs Division of the
Department of the Army. Mr. Bachman of the Civil Affairs Division,
the officer directly responsible for this matter in the Department
of the Army, has stated firmly and repeatedly that there is no objec-
tion on the part of the Department of the Army to the distribution
of this document in the PEC. He has stated that he fesls it unneces-
sary to refer the matter to SCAP-OJEIRF. Mr. Hilliard has cleared
the distribution of the document with all interested offices of the
Department of State which have no objection. The Export-Impert Bank
officials have indicated they have no objection but have requested a
letter from the Department of State. This has been sent and a reply
received stating that the Export-Import Bank has no objection. Export-
Import Bank has stated, however, that private bankers should be con-
sulted. Mr. Stenger of ED has talked further with Export-Import Bank
CS/A
representatives and has expressed his own opinion, that it is unneces-
sary to consult private bankers. Mr. Bachman of CAD has made the same
statement to Mr. Koch. Under the circumstances I feal there can be no
objection whatever to the distribution of this document as a restricted
FEC document and have so informed Mr. Johnson by the attached letter.

MEMORANDUM FOR:
The Head of the United Kingdom Liaison Mission in
Japan
SUBJECT:
Japanese Pre-War Foreign Currency Bonds
1. Reference is made to the following memoranda to General
Headquarters, Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers, Civil Proper-
ty Custodian, from the United Kingdom Liaison Mission, subject,
"Japanese Pre-War Foreign Currency Bonds":
a. No. EA 3/221/48, 14 January 1948;
b. No. EA 7/221/48, 27 February 1948.
2. With reference to paragraph 2 of reference lb, no plan is
now under consideration for the repayment of debts in depreciated yen.
Resunption of service of pre-war bonds is not contemplated until an
over-all policy is adopted for the handling of foreign currency bonds.
Monies on deposit in the Special Property Administration Account repre-
senting irterest on foreign currency bonds will be so held pending
establishment of such policy and the determination of the ownership
of bonds.
3. A sunmary of information upon foreign currency bonds fur-
nished by the Japanese Government follows:
a. There was an amount of ¥69,638,156.53 in the Special
Property Administration Account in the Yokohama Specie Bank earmarked
"Fund for Interest on Foreign Obligations" which is now deposited
with the Bank of Japan. The Japanese Government has submitted de-
tails of this amount which represents the conversion into yen of
interest payments on Japanese Government, municipal and corporate
foreign currency obligations, sterling and U.S. dollars. The con-
version rate from dollars and sterling into yen varies from a little
under 10 to the pound to 16.84 to the pound and in the case of
dollars conversion from ¥4.25 to a little over ¥2 to the dollar.
These figures are set forth in Exhibit "A" enclosed. Exhibit "A"
gives the breakdown of each bond as to the dollar interest dates.
Exhibit "" gives the total interest payment in each series of bonds.
b. All of the payments made into this fund, with one ex-
ception, represented interest only and not sinking fund payments. The
exception is set forth opposite the figure 48 on the left side of the
Exhibit "A" which states "Sinking Fund for Redemption of Lottery Cer-
tificate" and the total of these two figures, namely £6,227 1/8 being
payment

=== Page 721 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 2 to Tokyo's 636,
September 28, 1948.
- 2 -
payment of principal of the Osaka 4% Harbor Construction Loan Bonds
which were redeemed by lot. In this connection see third page of
Exhibit "E".
c. On March 15, 1943 the Japanese Diet passed a law, No.
60, entitled "Law Relating to the Treatment of Foreign Bonds," a
translation of which is enclosed and marked Exhibit "B".
d. Also enclosed is a statement which goes into details as
to how these bonds and debentures were treated under this law (Ex-
hibit "C").
4. Briefly, the procedure was as follows and can be divided in-
to the following categories:
a. The Japanese Government called in for conversion all
Japanese external dollar and sterling bonds and issued in place there-
of yen obligations of the Japanese Government. This phase of conver-
sion covered all bonds physically located in Japan, but may not have
applied to foreien currency bonds owned by United Nations nationals
in Japan. It has been impossible to ascertain in detail to what ex-
tent this latter exemption applied.
b. The Japanese Govemment also converted into yen bonds
the sterling and dollar bonds which were located outside of Japan but
owned by persons in Japan. This conversion was made on the basis of
certification of the owners in Japan of such ownership and of the lo-
cation thereof out side Japan.
5. There is inclosed a schedule (xhibit "") which shows for
each series of bonds the bonds out standing at the outbreak of the war
and the bonds purchased or redeemed in accordance with the law re-
ferred to in 3c above and also the bonds outstanding after the con-
version into yen bonds was made.
6. The Japanese Government 4 sterling bonds of 1910 and the
Tokyo 5% sterling loan of 1912 contained a provision that the inter-
est could be paid in Swiss francs in Switzerland. Enclosed is a
schedule, Exhibit "F", showing the remittances made in Switzerland.
The officials of the Japanese Government state that these remittances
were made on the certification by the Japanese Minister in Switzerland
that the coupons or bonds owned by a neutral or a national whose
government was friendly to Japan during the war. They further stated
that so far as they know coupons which were paid in Switzerland have
not been returned to Japan nor does the Japanese Government have a
record of the owners of the coupons or bonds. Regarding 4b above, the
Japanese Government has been instructed to supply details of the
numbers and owners of bonds which were not in Japan during the war but
which the Japanese Government converted or paid to the owners in Japan
in yen. Attention of the Ainistry of Finance officials was called
to the fact that it was quite possible that Japanese foreign currency
bonds could be situated in New York or London and have been taken pos-
session of by the Alien Property Custodians in those countries and
yet the owners in Japan long since received yen for these bonds. The
Japanese Government officials admitted that this was so and when we
called

proximately $3,000,000 face value of 6-1/2% dollar bonds which were
on deposit in the New York Agency of the Yokohama Specie Bank and

The attsched report contain a considerable amount of

interesting material not merely on the question of MFN for

following is an abbreviated presentation of the U.S. and U.K.
08
positions at the conference derived from the Agreed Summary
6
and the U.S. Minutes, followed by quoted highlights from
Mr. Barnett's memoranda of private conversations with British
officials.
The essential American position as expressed by Ambassador
Douglas at the opening session was that the U.S. taxpayer 1s
bearing a heavy burden in Japan, that this burden reduces our
resources available for other purposes in the world, that the
drain in Jupen could only be reduced through expanded Japanese
trade, and th t MFN for Jepan would be of material assistance
25
toward that end.
€：
8
British questioning of the necessity or advisability of
according reciprocal MFN treatment to Japan at this time was
based on four main arguments:
1. MFN for Japan at this time is of purely academic im-
portance. The recently concluded sterling arrangement provides
for the maximum expansion of British Commonwealth-Japenese
trade now possible in the light of present and prospective
currency availabilities, and MFN could not increase that trade.
2. Japan wes recently a brutal enemy and is still hated
in Commonwealth countries. Any attempt to secure MFN now would
be popularly interpreted as a move for Japan's benefit (even
though it was appreciated by those present that it would really
Confid
be more for U.S. than Japanese benefit for some time to come)
and would be cxplosive politically.
3. It would be dangerous to grant MFN to Japon until the
Japanese price structure and cost factors have become better
len
known. Japan's expanded and expanding population and difficult
economic situation create a serious danger that before very long
after the occupation Japen will again be forced to resort to
工
HH
dumping
ile

dumping and other predatory trade practices to survive. British
industry's fear of revived Japenese competition of this type
should not be underestimated.
4. It would be difficult if not entirely impractical to
accord MFN to Japn under its present "multiple exchange rate"
system. Establishment of & commercial exchange rate for the
yen must be considered a virtual prerequisite to the grenting
of yFN by the Commonwealth countries.
